**Summary.** This paper introduces Oracle Distillation, a protocol designed to purify an unknown, noisy quantum oracle by querying it many times. It proves that this distillation process can successfully protect quantum advantages for key algorithms, such as Grover search, even when the error rate is non-zero but below a critical threshold. This opens a path for robust quantum sensing and computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a robust theoretical framework for quantum sensing and machine learning by showing how quantum advantages can be maintained against realistic noise models, which is crucial for experimental quantum information science.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is developing a method to preserve quantum computational advantages in learning and sensing tasks when the underlying system interaction (the oracle) is unknown and corrupted by noise.

**Main result.** The authors introduce Oracle Distillation, proving that quantum advantage for problems like Grover search is retained at low error rates, provided the noise is below a specific constant threshold.

**Method.** The core technique is Oracle Distillation (OD), which consumes many noisy queries to synthesize a single, high-fidelity oracle. This relies on using 'weak queries' to facilitate error correction while preserving necessary information.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by an unknown unitary 'oracle' acting as the interface between the quantum device and the external system. Noise is modeled using both adversarial corruption and i.i.d. depolarizing noise.

**Key observables.** Fidelity of the distilled oracle ($\epsilon$-closeness), quantum advantage over classical methods, and the threshold error rate ($p^*$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The per-qubit depolarizing rate ($p$), the required fidelity ($\epsilon$), and the query complexity ($TOD$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol assumes a universal quantum computer with ideal gates, focusing noise only on the oracle itself.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the OD process, showing noisy queries distilling to a high-fidelity oracle, and Figure 1b plots the advantage threshold versus noise parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of noise in unknown-oracle tasks, then constructs the OD protocol for adversarial noise, establishes its near-optimality, and finally proves a threshold theorem for i.i.d. depolarizing noise, extending the concept to continuous time.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Learning and sensing are among the most promising applications of quantum technology, often with provable quantum advantages. In any realistic experiment, however, noise threatens to erase these advantages as the problem size grows. We introduce oracle distillation, a procedure that distills many queries to a noisy oracle into a single high-fidelity oracle. Here the oracle, an unknown unitary from a known family, models the interface through which a quantum device learns about an unknown system. We construct an explicit protocol that distills any Boolean oracle, both under adversarial noise that corrupts a constant fraction of the input qubits and under i.i.d. depolarizing noise at a constant rate on every qubit. At the heart of the protocol is the weak query, in which the device deliberately queries the oracle only weakly, trading response strength for the ability to correct errors. Our protocol is near-optimal among all protocols that distill the oracle while keeping its errors correctable, revealing a fundamental tension between these two requirements. An important implication of the protocol is a threshold theorem: every Boolean oracle problem whose quantum advantage is polynomial in the domain size, including Grover search, $k$-forrelation, and Simon's problem, retains a quantum advantage whenever the per-qubit depolarizing rate is below a constant threshold. In particular, the quadratic quantum advantage of Grover search, long believed fragile under noise, survives nearly intact at low error rates even when almost every query involves errors on one or more qubits. We further extend the protocol to fractional and continuous-time Boolean oracles. Quantum error correction can thus protect not only prescribed operations but also unknown dynamics. Our results make a large family of quantum advantages in learning robust against noise and open a path toward robust computational sensing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents Stabilized Analytic Continuation (SAC) as a robust method to calculate the von Neumann entropy from limited integer Rényi entropy measurements. The authors provide deep theoretical insights into the limitations of this process, particularly identifying how zeros of the trace function $	ext{Tr} ho^z$ can cause analytic continuation to fail, leading to observable phase transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework connecting entanglement entropy calculation to complex analysis (RKHS theory) provides powerful, general tools for analyzing quantum correlations in strongly correlated systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is extracting the physically meaningful von Neumann entropy ($S_{	ext{vN}}$) from numerical data that only provides access to integer Rényi entropies ($S_n$).

**Main result.** The authors developed and rigorously analyzed Stabilized Analytic Continuation (SAC), showing it is robust but can fail when the Rényi function develops singularities (Fisher zeros) due to zeros of $	ext{Tr} ho^z$.

**Method.** The work combines analytic continuation techniques, mapping the problem onto a Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS) framework, and deriving universal bounds on the location of singularities based on spectral properties.

**Model / system.** The analysis is applied to general many-body quantum systems, benchmarking performance on the toric code, Kagome Heisenberg models, and $(1+1)d$ compact-boson CFTs.

**Key observables.** Von Neumann entropy ($S_{	ext{vN}}$), mutual information, integer Rényi entropies ($S_n$), and the function $	ext{Tr} ho^z$ in the complex $z$-plane.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The rank ($r$) of the density matrix, the number of input Rényi samples ($N$), and the location of zeros of $	ext{Tr} ho^z$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes a noiseless setting for the theoretical derivations, and the SAC method relies on the minimum-norm interpolant within a specific RKHS structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the SAC construction, the extraction of topological entanglement entropy for the Kagome model, and the reconstruction of mutual information across various physical benchmarks.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces SAC as a robust alternative to conventional extrapolation. It then provides theoretical derivations establishing zero-free domains for $	ext{Tr} ho^z$ and deriving bounds on singularities. Finally, it benchmarks SAC performance across three distinct physical settings: topological entropy, mutual information, and quantum dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Extracting operationally meaningful quantities such as von Neumann entropy and mutual information is central to characterizing many-body quantum systems, yet experiments and numerics often provide direct access only to integer Rényi entropies. Whereas conventional extrapolation relies on a prescribed fitting ansatz, in PRL 137, 100202 we introduced an alternative approach based on stabilized analytic continuation (SAC), which avoids such an ansatz and is inherently robust to noise. Here, we further develop this framework in the noiseless setting and benchmark its performance across several nontrivial many-body problems. We first show that, besides the intrinsic ambiguity of reconstructing the von Neumann entropy from finitely many Rényi samples, analytic continuation can also fail because of genuine nonanalyticities in the Rényi function arising from zeros of $\text{Tr}ρ^z$. We derive universal zero-free domains for $\text{Tr}ρ^z$ and systematically sharpen these bounds using additional spectral information about $ρ$. We then benchmark the performance of SAC in three settings: ($i$) extracting topological entanglement entropy from DMRG Rényi data for the toric code and Kagome Heisenberg models; ($ii$) reconstructing the mutual information between disjoint intervals in a $(1+1)d$ compact-boson CFT; and ($iii$) recovering finite-time ballistic growth of the von Neumann entropy from integer Rényi entropies that cross over toward subballistic growth in diffusive quantum dynamics. In the latter setting, analytic continuation is expected to fail in the long time limit. We illustrate this mechanism with a physically motivated two-sector model, where the asymptotic separation between von Neumann and higher-Rényi growth is accompanied by a zero of $\text{Tr}ρ^z$ pinching the real axis at $z=1$, thereby producing a first-order Rényi phase transition.

</details>

### [Informational and algebraic renormalization group](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30362v1)

**Authors:** Takato Mori, Teruaki Nagasawa  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.30362v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<sub>FIG. 1. RG as a quantum channel and its resource theory. (a) One RG step G = V ◦C coarse grains a state (C) and rescales it back to the original Hilbert space by an isometry (V). Iteration proceeds through the reduced map bG = C ◦V on the coarse-grained space. (b) Resource theory of RG. The Ces`aro mean G∞maps a UV state ρ to a fixed point in the set F(G) of free states (IR), even when the iterates Gn(ρ) do not converge. Free operations E commute with G∞and cannot increase the resource IG(ρ) = D(ρ∥G∞(ρ)).</sub>

**Summary.** This work unifies the Renormalization Group concept using quantum information theory, treating RG as a quantum channel. It develops an algebraic framework for coarse-graining and establishes a resource theory where fixed points are free states. The authors derive key physical metrics, such as the BKM metric, using relative entropy, providing a rigorous mathematical toolset for analyzing critical phenomena across different physical regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous connection between quantum information measures (relative entropy) and fundamental physical concepts like RG flow and thermal metrics (BKM) provides powerful new diagnostic tools for analyzing quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to formulate the Renormalization Group (RG) in a general quantum-information framework that extends beyond standard momentum-space, factorized settings, specifically addressing the information loss during coarse-graining.

**Main result.** They develop an algebraic RG framework and construct a resource theory of RG, showing that the relative entropy monotone quantifies the distance from the IR fixed point, and they derive the BKM metric for thermal states.

**Method.** The authors employ quantum information tools, treating RG as a quantum channel, defining the low-energy algebra via conditional expectation, and using relative entropy as a resource monotone.

**Model / system.** The analysis applies to general quantum systems, including finite-dimensional ones, and models quantum field theories (QFTs) undergoing RG flow, particularly focusing on thermal states parameterized by couplings.

**Key observables.** Relative entropy monotone ($I_G(ho)$), BKM metric ($\chi_{ij}$), and the complementary measure of discarded UV information.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($eta$), coupling parameters ($g_i$), and the scale/momentum cutoff ($\Lambda$ or $u$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumptions include the existence of a low-energy algebra constructed from the one-particle spectrum and the use of specific functional forms for the relative entropy calculation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates RG as a quantum channel (coarse-graining followed by rescaling) and its resource theory flow via the Cesàro mean.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first formulating the RG as a quantum channel (IRG), then developing an algebraic extension (ARG) using conditional expectation, establishing a resource theory, and finally deriving and comparing physical metrics like the BKM metric using relative entropy differentiation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Renormalization group (RG) is a core concept in physics from statistical mechanics to quantum field theory, yet its schemes differ widely between field theory and many-body physics. We formulate the Wilsonian RG as a quantum channel, whose Kraus representation yields pure conditional trajectories for pure inputs and recovers mixed-state flows upon averaging. We then develop an algebraic extension applicable beyond the usual momentum-space, factorized setting: the low-energy algebra is constructed from the one-particle spectrum, and coarse graining is a conditional expectation onto it. We further construct a resource theory of RG whose free states are fixed points. Under stated assumptions, the resulting relative-entropy monotone is proportional to $c-c_{\rm IR}$ to quadratic order along a single stable RG direction near a two-dimensional IR fixed point. We also introduce a complementary measure of the UV information discarded by coarse graining, establish its monotonicity for successive coarse-graining maps, and discuss its holographic realization.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a sophisticated mathematical framework to analyze heat transport in composite materials by treating the system's conductance through a 'conductance filtration'. It introduces the concept of dissipation share, which quantifies how much the overall heat flow is limited by specific physical mechanisms like particle interfaces or matrix bonds. This provides a powerful diagnostic tool for materials design.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on thermal physics, the use of persistent homology (filtration, barcode) and sensitivity analysis (logarithmic derivatives) provides advanced mathematical tools applicable to characterizing complex, disordered energy landscapes in quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to improve the understanding of thermal conductivity in particle-filled composites by moving beyond single effective quantity evaluations.

**Main result.** It establishes that the dissipation share is exactly the logarithmic sensitivity of the effective conductance, providing a diagnostic tool to pinpoint the limiting heat transfer mechanism.

**Method.** The authors introduce the conductance filtration, combining thermal conductance ($g_e$) as the filtration axis with persistence relative to the sink boundary.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is particle-filled composites, where heat transport is analyzed by decomposing the conductance of particle pairs into three types: contact, near-contact, and bond.

**Key observables.** Dissipation share ($	ext{P}_{	ext{share}}$), allocation of dissipation among branch types, and the idealisation ratio of each mechanism.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Conductance ($g_e$), effective conductance ($G_{	ext{eff}}$), and the sink boundary conditions ($	ext{src}, 	ext{snk}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The branch-conductance model assumes isothermal nodes, and the analysis relies on mapping the system onto a weighted graph structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the entire 'design loop' closing on a single graph representation, linking structure, branch conductances, filtration, and diagnosis.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a framework by first formulating the thermal conductance using three branch types, then introducing the conductance filtration and sink-relative persistence, culminating in the derivation of the dissipation share and a closed-form prediction for edge sensitivity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We formulate the thermal conductance $g_e$ of a particle pair as three branch types -- contact, near-contact and bond -- within a single construction, and we introduce the filtration that takes this $g_e$ as its axis (the conductance filtration) together with persistence taken relative to the sink boundary. We then show that the dissipation share is exactly the logarithmic sensitivity of the effective conductance, and that a finite change of a single edge is predicted exactly in closed form. The allocation of the dissipation among branch types, and the idealisation ratio of each mechanism (interface, matrix and particle), are thereby obtained as diagnostic quantities on one and the same graph representation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the Mean First Passage Time for diffusion in a bounded 2D domain subjected to boundary-induced resetting. It compares this time to the case without resetting, showing that the benefit of resetting is highly dependent on the geometry and the proximity of the reset point to the target. The methodology combines renewal theory with advanced asymptotic analysis.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a rigorous theoretical framework for non-equilibrium dynamics in confined spaces, which is relevant for understanding transport phenomena in mesoscopic systems or engineered quantum environments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how boundary-induced resetting affects the Mean First Passage Time (MFPT) for a particle diffusing in a bounded 2D domain containing absorbing targets.

**Main result.** Boundary-induced resetting only reduces the MFPT compared to a reflecting boundary if the particle's reset point is sufficiently close to the target(s), unlike some simpler 1D cases.

**Method.** The analysis employs renewal theory, matched asymptotic expansions, and Green's functions to calculate the unconditional MFPT.

**Model / system.** The model is single-particle diffusion in a 2D bounded domain ($\Omega$) with absorbing interior targets and a sticky exterior boundary ($\partial\Omega$) that causes resetting to a fixed interior point $x_0$.

**Key observables.** Unconditional Mean First Passage Time (MFPT) for absorption by any target.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The mean waiting time ($	au$) at the sticky boundary, the initial position ($x_0$), and the geometry of the domain ($\Omega$) and targets.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Targets are assumed to be small and well-separated from each other and the exterior boundary; the analysis often relies on approximations for the leading order behavior.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate contour plots showing the MFPT and the sign of the benefit function ($\chi$) for single and dumbbell target configurations in 2D, comparing resetting effects in 1D vs 2D.

**Paper structure.** The paper first analyzes the simpler 1D interval case, then generalizes the methodology to the 2D domain $\Omega$, deriving the necessary renewal equations and calculating the MFPT using advanced mathematical techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Most studies of stochastic search processes with resetting focus on spontaneous resetting events that occur at a random sequence of times typically generated by a Poisson process. One of the major consequences of spontaneous resetting is that it yields a finite mean first passage time (MFPT) in unbounded domains, resulting in an optimal resetting rate that minimises the MFPT. An optimal resetting rate can also occur in bounded domains provided that the reset point is not too far from the target. Recently, there has been growing interest in event-based rather than spontaneous resetting, where resetting is triggered when a specific threshold is reached. Identifying the threshold with a physical non-target boundary then leads to so-called boundary-induced resetting. In this paper, we explore the effects of boundary-induced resetting on single-particle diffusive search in a two-dimensional (2D) bounded domain $Ω$ containing one or more small interior targets. The interior target boundaries are totally absorbing, whereas the exterior boundary $\partial Ω$ is sticky. That is, whenever the particle reaches a point on $\partial Ω$ it remains attached for a random waiting time $τ$ after which it immediately resets to a fixed interior point $\x_0\in Ω$. Using renewal theory, matched asymptotic analysis and Green's functions we calculate the unconditional MFPT for absorption by any of the targets and compare this with the corresponding MFPT of a search process on the same domain with a reflecting surface $\partial Ω$ and no resetting. We show that boundary-induced resetting only reduces the MFPT relative to the no resetting case if the searcher's reset point $\x_0$ is sufficiently close to one of the targets.

</details>

### [Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill error-correction with decohered resources](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31462v1)

**Authors:** Rajendra S. Bhati, T. Bazylewicz, Jarosław K. Korbicz  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.31462v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.31462_figures/2609.31462_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>

Homodyne outcomes are fixed to (0, 0) in each round, so that no unitary correction is applied. (b) Uhlmann fidelity of teleportation as a function of the dimensionless time ωct for the input state |0β⟩, with homodyne outcomes (0, 0), for varying values of γT/ωc and a Lorentz–Drude cutoff, Eq. (7). The results for the exponential cutoff, Eq. (8), are qualitatively similar. (c) Leakage for the same parameters as in (b). (d) Wigner functions of the +1 eigenstate of the GKP Pauli operator ˆσy, with β = 0.1 and τ = 0.5, after n = 0, 1, 2, 6, 10...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the performance of GKP error correction when the necessary entangled ancillary resources suffer from environmental decoherence. The authors show that this decoherence transforms the error correction into a correlated noise process that causes the encoded quantum state to leak out of the logical subspace. This finding is critical for designing robust quantum computing architectures based on bosonic modes.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial, non-trivial extension to fault-tolerance theory by quantifying how realistic environmental noise fundamentally breaks the self-correcting nature of leading bosonic quantum error-correcting codes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes how environmental decoherence affects the Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) error-correction protocol, which typically assumes ideal ancillary resources.

**Main result.** Under pure-dephasing, the GKP protocol degrades into a correlated Pauli-Gaussian noise process, meaning the code fails to self-correct and the encoded state leaks out of the logical subspace over successive rounds.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Quantum Brownian Motion (QBM) model to derive the decoherence factor and the resulting noisy teleportation Kraus operator, quantifying leakage and fidelity degradation.

**Model / system.** The system involves bosonic quantum states undergoing GKP error correction, modeled with a system-bath interaction Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}_{int}$) coupling to a bath of harmonic oscillators.

**Key observables.** Leakage of the encoded state out of the logical subspace ($L_n$), and the Uhlmann fidelity ($F$) of the encoded state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Decoherence strength ($\gamma$), cutoff frequency ($\omega_c$), and the number of correction rounds ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The decoherence is modeled using the QBM framework, focusing on the pure-dephasing limit, and the analysis assumes the ancillary states are subject to environmental noise.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the accumulation of leakage ($L_n$) versus the number of rounds, and the decay of Uhlmann fidelity and leakage versus dimensionless time for different environmental spectral densities.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of decoherence in GKP-EC, derives the decoherence factor using QBM, characterizes the resulting noise as correlated Pauli-Gaussian noise, and finally demonstrates the failure of self-correction via leakage accumulation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Teleportation-based Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) error correction typically assumes a decoherence-free GKP ancilla Bell pair, an idealization that is difficult to reconcile with the ubiquitous environmental decoherence in bosonic systems. Here, we analyze the performance of the GKP protocol subject to pure-dephasing processes, ubiquitous in realistic situations. We show that, under this class of interactions, teleportation, instead of projecting the input onto the GKP subspace, transforms GKP error correction into a correlated Pauli-Gaussian noise process that can accumulate over successive correction rounds. Thus, unlike in previously studied noisy GKP scenarios, the code no longer self-corrects. Moreover, repeated noisy GKP teleportation drives the encoded modes out of the logical subspace, progressively degrading the encoded codewords. We show that the resulting leakage rapidly saturates with the number of correction rounds. Finally, we investigate the implications of our results for motional-oscillator-based GKP error-correction platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This work determines the precise conditions under which a memory-limited Quantum Extreme Learning Machine can replace a quantum reservoir computer. The central finding is that perfect emulation requires the influence of the omitted past history to vanish uniformly, establishing a fundamental limit on what finite-window models can reconstruct about a system's true history.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous theoretical boundary for using machine learning models (QELMs) to simulate physical systems with inherent memory (QRCs), which is crucial for understanding the limits of quantum machine learning in physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental conditions under which a memory-less Quantum Extreme Learning Machine (QELM) can accurately emulate the temporal memory retention capabilities of a Quantum Reservoir Computer (QRC).

**Main result.** Accurate emulation is possible if and only if the influence of the omitted past history becomes uniformly negligible as the input window grows, otherwise, a persistent error floor remains regardless of model capacity.

**Method.** The analysis compares the QRC output to the QELM prediction by quantifying the worst-case output variation ($D_d$) when the input window is fixed but the past history varies.

**Model / system.** The models involve quantum reservoir dynamics (QRC) and reset-window QELMs operating on quantum states ($ho_t$). The dynamics are governed by input-dependent Hamiltonians and local dissipation terms.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the error floor $D_d$, which quantifies the output variation due to missing history; other observables include the trace distance ($	ext{D}_{	ext{tr}}$) and the joint spectral radius ($	ext{r}_{	ext{vis}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Window length ($d$), the decay rate characterized by $	ext{r}_{	ext{vis}}$, and the error tolerance ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes exact expectation values and focuses on the theoretical limits of representation capability, distinguishing this from practical training efficiency.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically compares QRC memory retention (state evolution) versus QELM window usage, illustrating cases where memory influence vanishes versus where a gap persists.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework by defining the memory difference between QRCs and QELMs, deriving the necessary condition for emulation ($	ext{D}_d 	o 0$), and then providing rigorous bounds and counterexamples using specific quantum models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum reservoir computers (QRCs) retain information from earlier inputs, whereas reset-window quantum extreme learning machines (QELMs) start afresh and receive only a recent input window. We establish when this window can replace the reservoir's memory. Under our stated assumptions and with exact expectation values, sufficiently expressive QELMs can reproduce reservoir outputs arbitrarily accurately as their windows grow precisely when the influence of the omitted past becomes uniformly negligible. However, longer windows do not always solve the problem. We construct a simple two-qubit reservoir that performs temporal recall exactly, while every fixed-window predictor faces the same positive worst-case error over unbounded delays, regardless of model capacity. Analytical benchmarks and simulations distinguish missing history from limited features and finite measurements. The central conclusion is simple: better representations can improve how available information is used, but cannot recover a past the model never receives.

</details>

**Summary.** This review outlines a modern framework for designing 'metageometric' materials by simultaneously considering the interplay between a material's physical shape, its electronic band structure, and its magnetic spin textures. It moves beyond simple micromagnetics by incorporating geometric constraints and strain effects, promising new avenues for energy-efficient nanoelectronics and sensing.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily condensed matter/materials science, the explicit coupling between geometry (topology/curvature) and quantum-like order parameters (magnetization) echoes concepts in geometric phases and topological quantum matter, which are relevant to AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need to design novel functional materials by coupling three fundamental aspects: geometric shape, electronic band structure, and magnetic spin textures.

**Main result.** It proposes that accounting for the explicit coupling between geometry, strain, and electronic degrees of freedom allows for the design of 'metageometric' materials with controlled anisotropic and chiral responses.

**Method.** The review synthesizes concepts from differential geometry, mean-field micromagnetics, and solid-state physics to model these coupled effects.

**Model / system.** The focus is on thin magnetic shells and curved geometries (e.g., bent shells, N-tori), where the local magnetic order parameter is influenced by the manifold's curvature and induced strain.

**Key observables.** Curvature-induced anisotropy, topological constraints on magnetization (soliton number), magnetoelectric coupling, and strain-gradient effects.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nanoscale dimensions, curvature ($\kappa_u, \kappa_v$), strain ($\epsilon_{ij}$), and topological invariants.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The review extends beyond contemporary mean-field micromagnetics to incorporate lattice structure and electronic degrees of freedom, though strain gradient effects are noted as areas for further development.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the historical development of curvilinear magnetism, geometric curvatures, and the topological constraints on magnetization fields in various curved geometries.

**Paper structure.** The review progresses from defining the limitations of current micromagnetics to detailing the mathematical framework using differential geometry, and finally outlining the resulting novel physical phenomena and technological prospects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

To enable novel concepts in nanotechnologies, it is insightful to design materials with qualitatively new performances originating from recent discoveries in fundamental material science and physics. Curvilinear magnetism emerged as a tool to design chiral and anisotropic responses of materials at the nanoscale using the effects of the geometric shape and topology of the object. This concept is distinct yet complementary to material screening, which is primarily based on the optimization of intrinsic microscopic properties to modify magnetic textures for specific applications. Contemporary curvilinear magnetism focuses on mean-field micromagnetics, while the explicit contribution of the lattice structure and electronic degrees of freedom remains beyond consideration. In this review, we will emphasize on the effects which appear when this coupling is accounted for and that have resulted in novel concepts for the design of ``metageometric'' materials. Further development of this field will provide appealing technological prospects for fundamental understanding of curvilinear magnetic nanostructures and their applications in energy efficient and scalable nanoelectronics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a quantum circuit simulation framework to model three-flavor neutrino oscillations under various physical conditions, including vacuum, matter effects, and decoherence. It rigorously verifies the simulation against known analytical results while also calculating quantum information diagnostics like entanglement and CP asymmetry. The paper establishes a reproducible quantum pipeline connecting particle physics observables to quantum information theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit use of quantum circuits to model fundamental particle physics processes, combined with the calculation of quantum information measures, provides a novel computational bridge between particle physics phenomenology and quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper simulates three-flavor neutrino oscillations across various astrophysical environments (vacuum, matter, supernova) using quantum circuits.

**Main result.** The quantum circuit framework successfully reproduces analytical results for oscillation probabilities and establishes a quantitative link between low-energy oscillation observables and quantum-information measures like mode entanglement and CP asymmetry.

**Method.** The evolution is simulated by encoding the flavor space into a two-qubit system and applying time evolution via quantum circuits, utilizing Suzuki-Trotter splitting for time discretization.

**Model / system.** The system is three-flavor neutrino oscillation governed by a Hamiltonian that includes vacuum terms and density-dependent matter potentials. The simulation is extended to open quantum systems using a Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Oscillation probabilities ($P_{\alpha	oeta}$), mode entanglement (Concurrence), and CP asymmetry ($\Delta P$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** NuFIT 5.3 parameters, matter potential $A(r)$, and simulation parameters like Trotter order and step number.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The $|	ext{11}angle$ sector is assumed inert, and the simulation uses a minimal two-qubit encoding, excluding neutrino-neutrino self-interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the successful reproduction of vacuum oscillation results to high precision, the resonance behavior in MSW matter, and the convergence rates of the Trotter approximation.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically verifies the simulation across three regimes: vacuum propagation, constant-density MSW matter, and varying-density supernova shock-shell propagation. It then extends the model to include quantum information diagnostics and open-system decoherence.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We simulate three-flavor neutrino oscillations on two-qubit quantum circuits. The flavor space is embedded as $\ket{00}=ν_e$, $\ket{01}=ν_μ$, $\ket{10}=ν_τ$ with the $\ket{11}$ channel kept inert, and the input mixing parameters (NuFIT~5.3, normal ordering) follow the global fit. We verify three physical settings against analytic or exact references: vacuum propagation, reproduced by a mass-eigenstate phase circuit to $\sim 10^{-16}$; constant-density Mikheev--Smirnov--Wolfenstein (MSW) matter effects, showing resonant flavor conversion when the matter potential $A$ crosses $Δm^2_{31}$; and varying-density (supernova shock-shell) propagation by slicing plus second-order Suzuki--Trotter splitting, agreeing with exact evolution to $\sim 10^{-6}$. We report a Suzuki--Trotter precision--resource calibration varying order, step number, and matrix/probability error. It delimits brute-force Trotterization on solar baselines: we quantify both standard alternatives, coherence averaging and adiabatic MSW. We also compute quantum-information diagnostics from the same model parameters, namely mode entanglement and the CP asymmetry. Finally, we extend the framework to open quantum systems via a Lindblad master equation with dephasing and absorptive channels. These results provide a reproducible link between low-energy oscillation observables and quantum-information measures.

</details>

### [The memory equation of a characteristic function in surface diffusion: a sum rule](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31344v1)

**Authors:** S. Miret-Artés  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.31344v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.31344_figures/2609.31344_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: The two sets of quantities, drawn on one and the same decay and described in the text. The solid curve is the exact intermediate scattering function of an admissible relaxation spectrum, chosen arbitrarily and normalized so that Ω= 1; it stands for any admissible decay, no model of the adlayer entering it. The origin of the time is that of the diffusive regime, the motion within a well having been averaged over. (a) What the theory fixes, the shaded area being the correlation time τc(K). (b) What a measurement returns, over the window shown.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous theoretical framework to describe the decay dynamics of density waves in adlayers using the Mori-Zwanzig formalism. It establishes an exact memory equation and a corresponding sum rule that connects the measured decay rate to static structural properties and single-jump probabilities. This framework successfully predicts the known hydrodynamic limit (Darken relation) while remaining general for various interaction models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous derivation of transport relations (like the Darken relation) from fundamental memory function theory, applicable to many systems exhibiting diffusive dynamics, provides a powerful theoretical tool for analyzing relaxation spectra.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Deriving an exact memory equation for the decay of a density wave in an interacting adlayer and establishing a general sum rule governing the local rate.

**Main result.** The local rate obeys a sum rule relating its momentum dependence to the single-jump characteristic function and the static structure factor. In the hydrodynamic limit, this reduces to a Darken-type relation.

**Method.** The theory employs the Mori-Zwanzig projection formalism to derive the memory equation, utilizing detailed balance and equilibrium averages to constrain the coefficients.

**Model / system.** The system is an adlayer undergoing surface diffusion on a crystal surface, modeled by dynamics governed by a master equation for the pair correlation function.

**Key observables.** Intermediate Scattering Function (ISF), Static Structure Factor (SSF), Local Rate, Effective Hop Rate, and the ratio $R(K)$ derived from moment analysis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Wavevector $K$, correlation time $	au_c$, and the long-wavelength limit ($K 	o 0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics must satisfy conservation, fixed jump sets, detailed balance, and translational invariance (H1-H4).

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes reference a Table 1 showing measured values for amplitude, rate, and other diagnostic quantities across various wavevectors.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general Mori equation for the ISF, derives the sum rule connecting the decay rate to single-jump correlations, and then analyzes the long-wavelength limit to recover known transport relations like the Darken equation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The decay of a density wave in an interacting adlayer obeys an exact memory equation of Mori's form. Being a Fourier component of a conserved density, its correlation function is a characteristic function, and the kernel is built from a second one, that of a single jump. The local rate obeys a sum rule whose momentum dependence is one minus the characteristic function of a single jump, divided by the static structure factor. Detailed balance makes the decay a superposition of relaxation modes of non-negative weight whose moments are static equilibrium averages, so that the truncations of the associated continued fraction form a convergent hierarchy of closed forms, each of them a bound on the decay itself. The single rate a fit returns is shown to be the mean of the instantaneous rate over a time window, hence never above the local rate. At long wavelength, the two coincide, the relaxation spectrum separating there, one collective mode taking almost the whole weight of the density wave and the single exponential usually fitted to spin-echo data being the decay itself. What an experiment reaches at every wavevector is also the area under the decay, a correlation time. Together with the static structure factor and the single-jump geometry, it forms one combination which the theory identifies with the effective hop rate divided by the product of the local rate and the correlation time. That product never falls below unity and tends to unity at long wavelength whenever the rate at which an adparticle leaves a site is independent of the site it leaves for. In the hydrodynamic limit, the sum rule becomes a relation of Darken type in which the rate multiplied by the thermodynamic factor is the effective hop rate and not the mobility of a labelled adparticle.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a 'Colored Weingarten Calculus' to analyze quantum systems constrained by symmetries, modeled by the Block-Unitary Ensemble. It develops a mathematical framework showing that the system's dynamics are governed by color-preserving permutations rather than standard permutations. This has direct applications to understanding thermalization in many-body systems and quantum information theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful, general mathematical tools (Weingarten Calculus) to analyze thermalization and quantum scrambling in many-body systems that possess underlying symmetries, bridging quantum information theory and condensed matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a constructive Weingarten Calculus for the Block-Unitary Ensemble to provide a unified mathematical framework for systems where quantum randomness is constrained by symmetries.

**Main result.** The calculus reveals a symmetry-resolved Schur-Weyl duality, showing that the average of operators is governed by color-preserving permutations, leading to a block-diagonal structure in the Gram matrix.

**Method.** The authors calculate Haar moments and Gram matrix elements by analyzing the commutant of the collective unitary action, generalizing permutation group theory to color-preserving permutations.

**Model / system.** The model is the Block-Unitary Ensemble, $U(H|M) = U(H_1) 	imes \cdots 	imes U(H_m)$, which describes quantum systems whose Hilbert space is decomposed into independent symmetry-resolved subspaces. This is motivated by contexts like the Eigenstate Thermalization Hypothesis (ETH).

**Key observables.** The $k$-th order twirling map $\Phi^{(k)} U(H|M)(O^{\otimes k})$ and the resulting correlation functions $\langle O^U_{ij} angle$ are key observables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of subspaces ($m$), the replica number ($k$), the dimension of each subspace ($d_i$), and the occupation vector $\mathbf{n}$ are crucial parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the system dynamics can be effectively modeled by independent Haar-random unitaries acting on symmetry-constrained subspaces, allowing for the use of the Block-Unitary Ensemble.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the block-unitary matrix structure (Fig. 1) and the block-diagonal factorization of the Weingarten matrix based on the occupation vector (Fig. 2).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the Block-Unitary Ensemble and its commutant (Chunk 2), then deriving the general form of the Gram matrix elements using color-preserving permutations (Chunk 3), and finally applying this calculus to calculate correlation functions in the energy eigenbasis (Chunk 4).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Haar randomness is the standard null model for quantum typicality, scrambling, and random-matrix formulations of thermalization. In many physical settings, however, randomness is naturally constrained to subspaces of Hilbert space. This structure arises, in particular, both in systems with symmetry-resolved sectors and in the Eigenstate Thermalization Hypothesis (ETH), where mesoscopic energy windows define subspaces in which nearby energy eigenstates are mixed. Motivated by these settings, we consider a Block-Unitary Ensemble consisting of independently Haar-random unitaries acting on each subspace. We develop a constructive Weingarten Calculus for this ensemble, providing a unified framework across quantum information and many-body physics. This construction reveals a symmetry-resolved Schur-Weyl duality, where the relevant commutant is not the ordinary permutation algebra, but the one of color-preserving permutations. We apply this framework to quantum states under symmetry constraints and to energy-resolved ensembles for ETH.

</details>

### [Exact series formulas for the capacities of the amplitude damping channel](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31609v1)

**Authors:** Stefano Pirandola

**Summary.** This paper provides exact, convergent series formulas for calculating the quantum, classical, and entanglement-assisted capacities of the qubit amplitude damping channel. By employing advanced mathematical techniques, the authors characterize the limits of information transmission over this common noisy quantum channel. The results offer crucial analytical benchmarks for quantum communication theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous, exact mathematical characterizations of information loss in a fundamental open quantum system (amplitude damping), which is crucial for understanding quantum communication protocols in realistic noisy environments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to derive exact, explicit series formulas for the various information-carrying capacities (quantum, classical, entanglement-assisted) of the qubit amplitude damping channel.

**Main result.** It provides convergent series representations for these capacities, characterizing the optimal input states and establishing analytical bounds for two-way assisted communication.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced techniques including Lagrange-Bürmann inversion, optimization of coherent information, and the use of normalized moment formalisms to derive closed-form series.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the qubit amplitude damping channel, $A_\gamma$, which models energy dissipation in a quantum system characterized by the damping probability $\gamma$.

**Key observables.** Quantum Capacity $Q(A_\gamma)$, Unassisted Classical Capacity $C(A_\gamma)$, Entanglement-Assisted Capacity $CE(A_\gamma)$, and the lower bound for two-way assisted capacity $	ext{RRCI}(\gamma)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The damping probability $\gamma$ ($0 \le \gamma \le 1$), and derived parameters like $k = \gamma/(1-2\gamma)$ and optimal input populations $u^*_\gamma$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often restricts optimization to diagonal input states ($ho_u$) and relies on the absolute convergence of the derived series representations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the provided notes, but the structure implies comparisons between derived capacity formulas and known limits at $\gamma=0$ and $\gamma=1/2$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives formulas for different capacities (Quantum, Classical, Entanglement-Assisted) using series expansions, establishes bounds for two-way communication, and analyzes the behavior in different regimes of the damping parameter $\gamma$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive explicit, absolutely convergent series formulas for the quantum, unassisted classical, and entanglement-assisted classical capacities of the qubit amplitude damping channel, eliminating residual optimizations and implicit roots. We also obtain analytical upper and lower bounds on two-way assisted quantum and private communication capacities by evaluating a balanced-squashing bound and optimizing the reverse coherent information. Our results characterize the optimal input populations, establish convergence and truncation properties, and provide analytical benchmarks for quantum and classical communication over dissipative channels.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the flatness-generalization relationship in a specific neural network model using advanced statistical mechanics techniques. By calculating the Hessian spectrum and generalization error analytically, the authors show that the correlation between flatness and generalization is highly conditional, depending critically on whether the network is over- or under-parameterized and the specific learning task.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of statistical physics tools (replica method, free energy calculations) to analyze generalization bounds in deep learning models provides deep theoretical insights into the relationship between local geometry (flatness) and global performance (generalization).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the controversial 'flatness-generalization relation' in neural networks, determining if a flat loss landscape at the minimizer implies good generalization performance.

**Main result.** The correlation between flatness measures (left/right edge, spectral mean) and generalization error strongly depends on the learning task (regression vs. classification) and the ratio of parameters to data points.

**Method.** The authors use advanced theoretical tools like the zero-temperature Gibbs formulation, Edwards-Jones formalism, and the replica method to analytically derive predictions for the Hessian spectrum and generalization error.

**Model / system.** The model studied is a teacher-student tree-committee machine, analyzed in the proportional high-dimensional limit. The analysis involves calculating observables related to the empirical loss and the Hessian matrix.

**Key observables.** Flatness measures (left edge, right edge, spectral mean) of the Hessian spectrum, generalization error ($\epsilon_{	ext{regr}}^g$, $\epsilon_{	ext{class}}^g$), and order parameters ($Q, q_{ab}^l, r_a^l$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dataset size ($\alpha$), regularization strength ($\lambda$), and the ratio of parameters to data points (overparametrized vs. underparametrized regimes).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the tractability of the teacher-student committee machine model and utilizes approximations like the 1RSB solution and the annealed ansatz in the replica method.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically represents the tree committee machine. Figure 2 shows theoretical predictions for test error, squared norm, and teacher-student overlap versus $\alpha$ and $\lambda$ for regression.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first setting up the model and defining observables, then employing the replica method to calculate the free energy and Hessian spectrum in the high-dimensional limit, and finally analyzing the correlation between flatness and generalization error across different learning tasks and parameter regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The flatness of the loss landscape at a minimizer is a widely used heuristic for reasoning about neural-network generalization, yet evidence for this relation is mostly empirical and controversial. We study this relation in a teacher-student tree committee machine, where both the ERM estimator and the Hessian spectrum are analytically tractable in the proportional high-dimensional limit. First, we use a zero-temperature Gibbs formulation to obtain predictions for the observables of the typical minimizers of the empirical loss. Secondly, we use Edwards-Jones formalism to derive the limiting Hessian resolvent around these typical minimizers. All predictions agree with finite-size gradient-descent simulations. Finally, we study three measures of flatness, namely the left and right edges and the spectral mean, and check if a decrease in generalization error as the dataset size is increased corresponds to an increase in flatness. We find that the answer strongly depends on the learning task and on the ratio of the number of parameters to the number of data points. In regression, the spectral mean and right edge correlate with the generalization error, while the left edge does so only in the overparametrized regime. In classification this correlation reliably holds only in the highly overparametrized phase, while for underparametrized networks it can even reverse.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper generalizes the quantum Stein lemma to analyze the discrimination of quantum channels. It proves that the asymptotic error exponent in quantum hypothesis testing is governed by the minimum regularized Umegaki relative entropy over alternative channels. The work provides strong theoretical guarantees, including an exponential strong converse and finite-block bounds, applicable to various quantum processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous mathematical tools (Stein's Lemma) to quantify the distinguishability of quantum processes, which is fundamental for understanding channel capacity and resource quantification in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prove a generalized quantum Stein lemma for the parallel discrimination of a target quantum channel against families of alternative channels.

**Main result.** It establishes an asymptotic equivalence between the optimal type-II error exponent in hypothesis testing and the regularized Umegaki channel relative entropy minimized over the alternatives.

**Method.** The proof combines advanced techniques including uniform auxiliary map approximation, iterative reduction of the domination rate, and tensor amplification.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum channels (CPTP maps) acting on finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. Inputs can be entangled across multiple channel uses and with an auxiliary reference system.

**Key observables.** Type-I and Type-II error probabilities ($\alpha_n, eta_n$), the Umegaki relative entropy $D(ho\|\sigma)$, and the asymptotic testing exponent.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The error tolerance $\epsilon$, the number of uses $n$, and the rate $S$ (related to the entropy limit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The alternative channels must be compact and convex, closed under tensor products, and contain a faithful replacer for the main result to hold.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the model and main results in Section 2, details the testing minimax problem in Section 3, proves the exponential approximation/Stein identity in Section 4, and derives asymptotic consequences in Section 5.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove a generalized quantum Stein lemma for parallel discrimination of an arbitrary finite-dimensional channel against families of alternative channels. The alternatives are compact and convex, closed under tensor products, and contain a faithful replacer. At every fixed type-I error tolerance, the optimal type-II error exponent equals the regularized Umegaki channel relative entropy minimized over the alternatives, and both defining limits exist. Inputs may be entangled across channel uses and with a reference system. The main technical result constructs exactly trace-preserving approximations with exponentially small diamond error, dominated in completely positive order by free channels at every rate above the common exponent. The proof combines uniform auxiliary map approximation, iterative reduction of the domination rate, and tensor amplification. It yields asymptotic equipartition with trace-preserving smoothing at every subexponentially vanishing error, an exponential strong converse, and stability under vanishing diamond-norm perturbations. Under additional permutation invariance and closure under insertion of a fixed faithful input state and discarding of its output, we also prove an explicit finite-block completion bound. This quantitative construction uses weighted discarding, local operator corrections, and comparison with auxiliary extensions. Applications include state-preserving, coherence-restricted, covariant, entanglement-breaking, and positive-partial-transpose channels. For isometric targets against entanglement-breaking or positive-partial-transpose alternatives, we obtain exact finite-block testing and smoothing formulas.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the bound states in 2D Yukawa theory using advanced numerical techniques like Hamiltonian Truncation. By extending the theory to include necessary divergences, the authors map out the binding energy evolution across coupling strengths. The results provide non-perturbative insights into strongly interacting quantum field models, relevant for condensed matter physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The techniques developed—Hamiltonian Truncation and EFT extensions—are powerful, non-perturbative tools for analyzing strongly coupled quantum field theories, which is highly relevant for understanding strongly correlated electron systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on determining the energy spectrum, particularly the bound states, of two-dimensional Yukawa theory, especially in regimes where standard perturbation theory fails.

**Main result.** The authors successfully track the evolution of fermion-antifermion bound states from weak to strong coupling, finding agreement with finite-volume perturbation theory at weak coupling.

**Method.** The primary method employed is Hamiltonian Truncation (HT) and its extension, Hamiltonian Truncation Effective Theory (HTET), which is used to study the spectrum by restricting the Hilbert space up to a cutoff energy.

**Model / system.** The system is the two-dimensional Yukawa theory, described by a Lagrangian involving a scalar field ($\phi$) and a fermionic field ($\psi$). The theory can be related to the massive Thirring/Sine-Gordon models in certain limits.

**Key observables.** Binding energy ($E_B$) of fermion-antifermion bound states, and the energy spectrum of the system.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling constants ($g$), masses ($m_\phi, m_\psi$), and the energy cutoff ($E_{	ext{max}}$). The analysis explores weak, strong, and large $m_\phi$ coupling regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations rely on Hamiltonian Truncation, which is computationally limited by the exponential growth of the Hilbert space. The analysis also involves approximations like the non-relativistic limit and the large $m_\phi$ limit.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares finite-volume binding energy computed via NRPT, NLIE, and infinite-volume results for the Thirring/Sine-Gordon model. Figure 11 compares the perturbative result with Hamiltonian truncation results for the binding energy, showing cutoff dependence.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the general framework using HTET, then analyzing specific limits (like the non-relativistic and large $m_\phi$ limits) to calculate the binding energy, and finally comparing these results against established perturbative and finite-volume methods.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the spectrum of two-dimensional Yukawa theory using Hamiltonian truncation, with a focus on bound states. We extend the framework of Hamiltonian truncation effective theory to include fermions and logarithmic UV divergences. With these corrections, the energy levels converge as a function of the cutoff with the scaling predicted by power counting. We identify a strategy to follow the evolution of fermion-antifermion bound states from weak to strong coupling, and investigate its binding energy, finding good agreement with finite-volume perturbation theory at weak coupling. Finally, we explore the heavy-scalar regime toward the massive Thirring/Sine-Gordon limit, recovering the expected weak-coupling behavior and outlining how the calculation can be extended toward this limit.

</details>

### [COFI-DQI: Curve-based Optimal Function Intersection via Decoded Quantum Interferometry](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31484v1)

**Authors:** Gretchen L. Matthews, Julia Shapiro  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.31484v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<img src="2609.31484_figures/2609.31484_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1. Comparison of DQI and Prange for Suzuki one-point codes over F8 in the balanced case r = q/2 = 4. Panel (a) plots the expected satisfaction fraction s/n, while panel (b) plots the advantage sDQI/n −sPrange/n.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops COFI, a generalized framework for applying Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI) to solve complex optimization problems over finite fields. By analyzing various Algebraic Geometry codes, the authors prove that advanced code families, like Suzuki codes, offer superior performance guarantees compared to simpler constructions when used within the DQI quantum framework.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is on classical coding theory applied to quantum algorithms, the rigorous comparison of code structures and the derivation of performance bounds are highly relevant to understanding the resource scaling and limitations of quantum algorithms in quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the Maximum Linear Satisfiability (max-LINSAT) problem, framing it as a structured optimization task solvable via quantum decoding.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that using advanced Algebraic Geometry (AG) codes, such as Suzuki or two-point Hermitian codes, leads to a strictly larger expected performance improvement (DQI satisfaction fraction) compared to standard constructions like one-point Hermitian codes.

**Method.** The core method is Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI), which maps the classical optimization problem onto a syndrome-decoding problem for the dual of a linear code, leveraging quantum Fourier transforms.

**Model / system.** The theoretical model involves constructing quantum states $|P(f)\rangle$ whose measurement probabilities bias towards solutions that maximize the satisfaction of linear constraints defined over a finite field $\mathbb{F}_q$. The underlying structure is defined by algebraic geometry codes on projective curves.

**Key observables.** DQI Satisfaction Fraction ($s/n$), advantage over classical algorithms (e.g., Prange's algorithm), and the decoding radius ($\ell$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code parameters such as genus ($g$), length ($n$), and the field size ($q$); the comparison between different code families (Suzuki vs. Hermitian).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the ability to uniquely decode errors up to a certain weight $\ell$ using the dual code, and the comparison results are contingent on suitable assumptions regarding the divisor degree.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the expected satisfaction fraction and the advantage of DQI versus Prange's algorithm for Suzuki codes over $\mathbb{F}_8$. Tables compare satisfaction bounds for various code families.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon the connection between max-LINSAT and decoding theory. It introduces COFI as a generalization of OPI, then systematically compares the performance of different AG code families (Extended Norm-Trace, Suzuki, Two-point Hermitian) against established benchmarks (Hermitian, Reed-Solomon) using explicit propositions and examples.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In 2025, Jordan et al. introduced Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI), a quantum algorithm for combinatorial optimization based on decoding. They considered Reed-Solomon decoding and its associated optimization problem, called Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI), which may be viewed as a polynomial regression problem over a finite field. DQI exhibits provable speedups on certain problem instances and establishes a connection between decoding problems and optimization tasks. Leveraging the well-understood dual structure and decoding theory of algebraic geometry codes from other curve families, we introduce COFI: Curve-based Optimal Function Intersection. By considering two-point Hermitian codes, Suzuki codes, and extended norm-trace codes, we broaden the range of algebraic geometry codes used in DQI and identify families that offer further improvements over one-point Hermitian codes in the Hermitian Optimal Polynomial Intersection framework considered by Jordan and Gu. Depending on the family and parameter regime, these curves can reduce quantum resource requirements or increase the number of constraints that can be considered.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a discrete analogue of Connes' distance to measure the geometric spread of quantum walks on lattices. By applying this metric to 2+1D and 1+1D models, the authors demonstrate that the distance quantifies how magnetic fields constrain propagation and how the metric naturally adapts to spatially varying geometries.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of Noncommutative Geometry tools like Connes' distance to quantum walks provides a powerful, mathematically rigorous way to quantify geometric effects (like magnetic fields or varying metrics) on quantum transport.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the geometric spread of discrete-time quantum walks by adapting Connes' distance formula from continuous spectral geometry to a discrete lattice setting.

**Main result.** The discrete Connes distance successfully measures wavepacket spread, showing that magnetic fields suppress long-time expansion, and the framework naturally captures inhomogeneous spatial metrics.

**Method.** The authors adapt the Connes distance, defined via spectral triples, by replacing the continuous Dirac operator with a discrete unitary step operator, analyzing the resulting commutator norms.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on discrete-time quantum walks (DTQWs) simulating systems ranging from free particles in (2+1)D space to walks on lattices simulating inhomogeneous metrics in (1+1)D.

**Key observables.** Connes Distance ($d_D$), dynamical spread of Gaussian wavepackets, and the commutator norm of the step operator.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensionality (1+1D, 2+1D), magnetic field strength (gauge-induced phases), and the time step $\epsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the convergence of the discrete step operator to the continuous Dirac Hamiltonian in the limit $\epsilon 	o 0$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the discrete analogue of the Connes distance, then applying it to analyze the 2+1D case (free vs. magnetic field), and finally extending the framework to model inhomogeneous metrics in 1+1D using convex optimization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the geometric spread of discrete-time quantum walks using Connes' distance formula. We introduce its discrete analogue by adapting the continuous spectral metric to the lattice, with the Dirac operator replaced by the unitary step operator. Focusing first on a (2+1)-dimensional quantum walk, we evaluate the dynamical spread of localized wavepackets to compare propagation in free space against that under a uniform magnetic field. We find that while the free walk shows linear ballistic growth, gauge-induced phases in the magnetic walk constrain the admissible test operators, suppressing the long-time geometric expansion. Furthermore, we apply this discrete framework to a (1+1)-dimensional plastic quantum walk simulating an inhomogeneous spatial metric. The results demonstrate that the discrete Connes distance naturally captures the underlying spatially varying metric, providing a robust measure of the wavepacket's spread that adapts directly to the local simulated geometry.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes a spin-1 Heisenberg chain model to study the emergence of true long-range order in a U(1) symmetric system. Using DMRG and CFT, the authors find numerical evidence supporting a critical point predicted by field theory. The key finding is that the divergence of the Luttinger parameter at this transition confirms the existence of long-range order, which is crucial for understanding quantum criticality in low dimensions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed numerical confirmation of a non-trivial quantum critical point where symmetry breaking occurs in 1D, offering insights into how dimensionality and interaction terms can circumvent strict theorems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the emergence of true long-range order in one-dimensional quantum spin chains with continuous U(1) symmetry, challenging standard theorems that forbid such order.

**Main result.** Numerical results confirm critical behavior predicted by Nahum's field theory, showing that the divergence of the Luttinger parameter and the critical exponent $\beta \approx 0.29$ support the existence of true long-range order at the quantum critical point.

**Method.** The study employs Density Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) simulations, combined with Conformal Field Theory (CFT) analysis, to calculate entanglement entropy and critical exponents.

**Model / system.** The system is an anisotropic spin-1 Heisenberg chain model incorporating biquadratic interactions, described by a specific Hamiltonian. The analysis maps the low-energy physics to a Luttinger liquid theory.

**Key observables.** Magnetization exponent ($\beta$), Luttinger parameter ($K$), central charge ($c$), and the dynamical critical exponent ($z_{eff}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling constants $g_1$ and $g_2$ define the phase diagram, with critical points $g_c^1$ and $g_c^2$ marking transitions between different ordered phases.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the system to a continuum field theory (Luttinger liquid) and comparing numerical results against $\epsilon$-expansion predictions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram, the scaling of entanglement entropy confirming $c=1$ for the Luttinger liquid, and the divergence of the Luttinger parameter $K$ near the critical point $g_c^2$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the phase diagram, first confirming Luttinger liquid behavior via entanglement entropy, then determining critical exponents ($\beta$) and the dynamical exponent ($z$) by analyzing finite-size scaling of excitation energies and the Luttinger parameter divergence.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a recent paper Nahum argued on the basis of a renormalization-group analysis that, contrary to standard lore, ground states of 1D spin chains with short-range interactions can spontaneously break U(1) ``easy-plane'' spin rotation symmetry. True long-range order of $(S^x,S^y)$ arises at the phase transition between two quasi-long-range-ordered phases. Here we present detailed numerical results for an anisotropic spin-1 chain model. We find a magnetization exponent $β\approx0.29$, consistent with the $ε$-expansion prediction $β\approx0.28$, a divergence of the Luttinger parameter on approaching the transition from both sides with an exponent that we relate to Nahum's transition, and finite-size spectra consistent with a dynamical critical exponent $z\approx2$. Our results support the critical behavior predicted by Nahum's field theory.

</details>

**Authors:** E. I. Kats  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.30916v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical work explains the emergence of nanoscale heliconical structures in liquid crystals by proposing that the transition is driven by the condensation of a short-range orientational order parameter. Using Landau theory, the authors show this mechanism is analogous to other known phase transitions, predicting the resulting modulated phase and associated Goldstone modes.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of a secondary, hidden order parameter condensing at a finite wave vector to drive a phase transition is a general mechanism in condensed matter physics, relevant to understanding modulated phases in complex systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the spontaneous formation of nanoscale periodic structures, specifically the heliconical modulation, observed in the twist-bend nematic phase of liquid crystals.

**Main result.** This modulation arises from the condensation of a secondary, short-range orientational order parameter ($\varphi$) at a finite wave vector ($\mathbf{q}_0$), rather than a simple loss of conventional nematic order.

**Method.** The analysis employs a Landau phenomenological theory, drawing analogies to established phase transitions like the de Gennes nematic-smectic-A transition.

**Model / system.** The system is liquid crystals (bent-core/dimeric molecules) exhibiting the N-NTB transition. The theory models the free energy using two order parameters: the long-range nematic director ($\mathbf{n}$) and the short-range correlation parameter ($\varphi$).

**Key observables.** Heliconical modulation pitch ($\Lambda = 2\pi/q_0$), the secondary order parameter ($\varphi$), and the associated Goldstone mode.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The wave vector $\mathbf{q}_0$, and parameters governing the effective potential (e.g., $\alpha, eta$ in the chiral case).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes the transition is driven by the condensation of $\varphi$ and predicts the transition is weakly first-order due to fluctuation effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Landau theory by first establishing the role of the secondary order parameter $\varphi$ condensing at $\mathbf{q}_0$. It then analyzes the resulting heliconical structure, drawing parallels to known phase transitions and discussing the implications of fluctuation corrections.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The discovery of the twist-bend nematic phase in liquid crystals composed of bent-core and dimeric molecules has revealed an unexpected mechanism for the spontaneous formation of nanoscale periodic structures in soft condensed matter. Unlike conventional liquid-crystalline phases, the twist-bend phase exhibits a nanoscale heliconical modulation despite the absence of molecular chirality. In this note, dedicated to the late R. Meyer, we discuss a Landau phenomenological interpretation of this phenomenon. The central idea is that a short-range orientational order parameter undergoes condensation, giving rise to a heliconical structure characterized by a finite wave vector. We emphasize that the conventional nematic order is already long-ranged, whereas the additional order parameter describes local orientational correlations hidden within the nematic state. The resulting phase transition bears a close analogy to the de Gennes theory of the nematic--smectic-A transition. Fluctuation effects are expected to drive the transition weakly first order. The theory also predicts a new Goldstone mode associated with the spontaneously broken continuous symmetry of the heliconical state

</details>
