**Summary.** This paper introduces RLQ-Grad, a novel reinforcement learning optimizer designed to train Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs). It overcomes the critical challenges of barren plateaus and exponential computational cost associated with calculating quantum gradients. By using a classical policy to propose updates, RLQ-Grad achieves massive speedups and maintains robust gradient variance across large qubit systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a novel, computationally tractable optimization framework for QML models, directly addressing the primary bottleneck (gradient calculation) that limits the practical application of variational quantum algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Training Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs) is severely limited by the barren plateau phenomenon (vanishing gradient variance) and the exponential computational cost of calculating quantum gradients.

**Main result.** The proposed RLQ-Grad optimizer bypasses explicit gradient computation by using a classical RL policy, achieving massive speedups and preserving gradient variance where traditional methods fail.

**Method.** The authors formulate QNN training as an RL problem, training a PPO agent to propose parameter updates directly, conditioned on the current state (parameters, loss, accuracy).

**Model / system.** The model involves QNNs built on hardware-efficient ansatz circuits using $n$ qubits and $L$ layers, trained for supervised quantum machine learning tasks.

**Key observables.** Gradient variance (showing decay for standard methods vs. flatness for RLQ-Grad), Top-1 validation accuracy, and computational time/memory complexity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits ($n$), number of layers ($L$), and the complexity scaling (linear in parameters vs. exponential in $n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The RL agent operates entirely in the classical domain, decoupling the optimization signal from the quantum circuit's intrinsic geometry.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare gradient variance across methods, and tables compare end-to-end memory and CPU time, demonstrating RLQ-Grad's superior resource efficiency.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of QNN training (barren plateaus, high cost). It then introduces RLQ-Grad, detailing the RL formulation, proving its theoretical advantages (gradient variance preservation, polynomial scaling), and finally benchmarking its performance against state-of-the-art gradient estimation techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Training quantum neural networks (QNNs) on near-term hardware remains hampered by two compounding difficulties: the exponential vanishing of gradient variance known as the barren plateau, and the $\mathcal{O}(L \cdot 2^n)$ time and memory cost of differentiating through an $n$-qubit, $L$-layer circuit. We propose RLQ-Grad, a reinforcement-learning-based optimizer in which a classical policy $π_φ$ (a spectrally-normalized PPO agent) learns to propose parameter updates directly, conditioned on the QNN's current parameters, loss, accuracy, and previous update. Because the surrogate gradient is emitted by a classical network rather than obtained by differentiating through the unitary $U(θ)$, its variance is not constrained by the barren plateau concentration bound, and its cost scales with the number of trainable parameters rather than the Hilbert-space dimension. We prove these properties formally and verify them on a hardware-efficient ansatz across four supervised benchmarks with up to $n=20$ qubits. RLQ-Grad preserves a near-flat gradient-variance curve where backpropagation, parameter-shift, and adjoint differentiation decay by 1 to 2 orders of magnitude. Accounting for the full training pipeline (PPO rollouts, actor-critic updates, and optimizer states), RLQ-Grad needs under 2 MB of memory and runs $2490\times$, $7876\times$, and $673\times$ faster per iteration than these three methods at $n=20$. It improves top-1 accuracy by up to $+10\%$ over gradient-based baselines on circuits of up to 12 qubits, and matches dedicated barren plateau mitigation methods on CIFAR-10 at 14 to 20 qubits, where evolutionary and gradient-free optimizers collapse to chance.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper derives the mathematical constraints on local quantum dynamics when those dynamics must respect relativistic principles like locality and causality. By generalizing concepts from factor systems to non-factor quantum algebras, the authors prove that the evolution must be linear, unitary, and block-decomposable. This work provides a rigorous framework for understanding quantum evolution in complex, non-factor quantum settings relevant to quantum gravity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The derivation of the required structure of quantum dynamics from fundamental principles like causality and locality is crucial for developing a consistent quantum theory of gravity or spacetime structure.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the constraints imposed by relativistic principles, specifically locality and the absence of superluminal signaling, on the local evolution of quantum systems.

**Main result.** For non-factor quantum systems, any compatible local evolution must be linear, unitary, and must respect the block decomposition of the system, leading to 'routed quantum evolutions'.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced mathematical formalism involving 'splitting maps' and 'generalised tensors' to characterize local operations in non-factor algebras.

**Model / system.** The system under study is a general quantum system described by non-factor algebras, relevant in theoretical contexts like quantum spacetimes and causal structures.

**Key observables.** The structure of the local evolution map ($L_\chi$), which must be linear and unitary.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the splitting maps ($\chi, 
u$) and the requirement of no superluminal signaling.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on generalizing concepts from factor systems and restricting the focus to canonical splitting maps.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from generalizing known results for factor systems to non-factor systems, defining local evolution via postulates (locality, no superluminal signaling), and culminating in a characterization theorem (Theorem 5.1) that dictates the form of the evolution map.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider the effect of relativistic principles on the structure of the local evolutions of non-factor quantum systems such as those which naturally arise in quantum spacetimes and causal structures. Generalising a former result that had been given for factor systems, we show that any evolution which can be applied locally on a direct sum of factor systems and without producing superluminal signals must be linear, unitary, and respect the block decomposition of the system.

</details>

**Authors:** Michael Williams de la Bastida, Peter V. Coveney  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.31502v1>

Data points in grey are random enumerations, orange are TOPP- HATT optimised, blue are hypergraph bisections using mt- KaHyPar and in red are (16n,8n,4) covering results.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, efficient method to parallelize the simulation of fermionic systems on multiple QPUs by optimizing the qubit encoding using combinatorial covering designs. By achieving a communication cost scaling of O(Mr), it significantly improves resource estimates compared to existing techniques. This advancement is crucial for scaling quantum simulations of molecular and condensed matter physics to larger systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the resource scaling challenges (communication overhead) inherent in simulating complex quantum many-body systems on near-term quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is efficiently parallelizing and distributing the Trotterized simulation of fermionic Hamiltonians across multiple Quantum Processing Units (QPUs).

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that using combinatorial covering designs achieves a communication cost scaling of O(Mr), which is superior to previous bounds and other optimization methods.

**Method.** They propose using combinatorial covering designs to define a minimal set of fermion-qubit encodings, allowing the Hamiltonian simulation to be partitioned and executed in parallel across multiple QPUs.

**Model / system.** The system involves fermionic modes governed by the Electronic Structure Hamiltonian (ESH), which is simulated via time evolution using the Trotter-Suzuki approximation.

**Key observables.** Communication cost (measured by the number of inter-QPU fSWAP gates) and the complexity scaling of the required encodings.

**Important parameters / regimes.** M (number of fermionic modes), r (Trotter number), and the specific structure of the Hamiltonian terms.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that all parallelized slices can be implemented without error, and it compares its performance against various encoding and partitioning strategies.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the qubit circuit diagram for parallelized first-order Trotterization using multiple circuit slices. Table I provides numerical comparisons of required encodings for various molecules using different methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by introducing the Trotterization of the ESH, detailing fermion-qubit encodings (like JW), introducing the concept of combinatorial coverings, and finally presenting the method for distributed fermionic Trotterization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We demonstrate a simple and efficient method to parallelize and distribute Trotterized Hamiltonian simulation of fermionic systems across multiple QPUs. Using combinatorial covering designs to define a minimal set of fermion-qubit encodings, we demonstrate communication cost scaling as $\mathcal{O}(M r)$ for a system of $M$ fermionic modes and Trotter number $r$, improving on the static encoding bound for $q$ QPUs, $\mathcal{O}(M^4 q r)$. We compare this approach to dynamic encoding using a randomised method, Pauli-weight based optimisation and hypergraph partitioning. Applying this to the Hamiltonians of a range of molecular systems, we find the combinatorial covering approach results in the lowest communication cost in all but the sparsest Hamiltonians.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper connects the geometry of planar contact structures to quantum computation by studying the Calabi-Yau condition on associated Stein fillings. The authors derive a criterion for this condition based on the factorization of a monodromy, showing that the topological constraints dictate the structure of the quantum state space. This suggests a fundamental link between contact topology and quantum entanglement.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep, mathematically rigorous link between advanced geometric topology (contact manifolds, Calabi-Yau geometry) and the fundamental structure of quantum computation, suggesting that topological constraints dictate quantum entanglement structure.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the relationship between the geometric properties of planar contact structures (specifically their associated Stein fillings) and the constraints imposed by topological quantum computation.

**Main result.** A criterion is established showing that the Calabi-Yau (CY) condition on the filling is equivalent to the existence of a specific 'trivialization' related to the factorization of the monodromy. Furthermore, the structure suggests that the choice of tensor product decomposition is determined by the lift, not just the braid group representation.

**Method.** The authors use techniques from contact topology, analyzing planar open books derived from lifting braids through branched covers, and applying algebraic criteria (like the trivialization condition) to determine the CY nature of the associated Stein fillings.

**Model / system.** The physical system involves planar open books derived from lifting braids through branched covers of the disk $D^2$. The quantum aspect is modeled via the Ising representation of the braid group acting on quantum state spaces, which relates to topological quantum computation.

**Key observables.** The first Chern class $c_1(W)$ (for the CY condition), the length of the factorization, and the structure of the resulting tensor product state space (e.g., qubits in the two-qubit doily).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree $d$ of the branched cover (with special attention paid to $d=3, 4, \ge 6$), and the specific monodromy $\phi$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the existence of a positive allowable factorization and the localization of the CY condition to subopenbook pieces. Uniqueness of the filling is only guaranteed for $d \le 3$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the geometric framework (planar open books, Stein fillings) and the CY criterion. It then analyzes the factorization structure, showing how the CY condition localizes to subcomponents. Finally, it connects this topological structure to quantum information by examining the resulting tensor product state spaces and the role of the lift versus the braid group representation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study planar open books obtained by lifting braids through branched covers of the disk D^2, together with the quantum operations in the Ising representation. We note a criterion for the associated Stein fillings to be Calabi-Yau (CY). Among positive factorizations of a fixed monodromy, every CY factorization has minimal length, and its filling minimizes χand b_2. An application to Baykur's recent examples gives one planar contact 3-manifold with infinitely many non-homeomorphic CY fillings. The cover there is of degree \ge 6. At degree 4 a single CY filling forces every filling to be CY, and the filling is unique in a certain case; at degree \le 3 the filling is unique and is CY under a mild condition. Admissible cuts of the covering disk decompose the openbook into subopenbooks. Every positive factorization then localizes to the pieces, and the CY condition holds exactly when it holds locally. This makes the state space a direct sum of tensor products indexed by the compatible parity choices with at most two qubits in each factor when the pieces have degree \le 4, which is also the range in which the CY condition depends only on the monodromy. For a particular degree 4 cover, the points, lines and flags of the two-qubit doily are realized by the system of its subopenbooks. Fifteen liftable braids there share the same quantum operation and the same Stein filling, and are separated only by which subopenbook systems they admit. A choice of tensor-product structure is thus carried by the lift and not by the braid group representation, which suggests a link between contact topology and quantum entanglement.

</details>

**Authors:** Sebastian O. M. Stewart, Nick Chancellor, Jonte R Hance, Ittoop Vergheese Puthoor  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.31077v1>

**Summary.** This paper presents a quantum-classical pipeline using QAOA to solve the NP-hard sidechain packing problem in protein folding, utilizing the backbone predicted by AlphaFold2. By developing a constraint-preserving ansatz with a ring mixer, the authors efficiently encode the energy minimization as a QUBO problem on qubits. The method successfully lowers the calculated conformational energy compared to the established AlphaFold baseline.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work demonstrates a sophisticated application of QAOA to a complex, classically hard problem in structural biology, specifically by engineering a novel, efficient ansatz (ring mixer) to manage qubit scaling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the NP-hard problem of sidechain packing in protein folding, which involves finding the lowest-energy rotamer assignment for sidechains attached to a fixed protein backbone.

**Main result.** The proposed hybrid quantum-classical pipeline successfully lowers the conformational energy compared to the AlphaFold baseline when tested on the 5PTI protein.

**Method.** It employs the Quantum Approximate Optimisation Algorithm (QAOA) to map the energy minimization problem onto a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimisation (QUBO) formulation.

**Model / system.** The system models protein sidechains, where rotamer assignments are encoded onto qubits using one-hot encoding. The optimization is performed on a Hamiltonian derived from the one- and two-body interaction energies.

**Key observables.** Conformational energy ($\Delta E$), the asymptotic shot-scaling metric ($S(N)$), and the cumulative success probability ($P_c(N)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit count ($N$), rotamer count ($R$), and the confidence level of the AlphaFold prediction ($	ext{pLDDT}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the backbone structure provided by AlphaFold2 is reliable enough to serve as a fixed scaffold, and it relies on a constraint-preserving ansatz to enforce one-hot validity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the reduction in gate scaling from $O(MN^2)$ to $O(MN)$ by using a cyclic ring mixer, and show the trend of the shot-scaling metric $S(N)$ versus qubit count for the 5PTI protein.

**Paper structure.** The paper details a multi-step pipeline: preprocessing with PyRosetta to define flexible regions, followed by QAOA optimization using a specialized constraint-preserving ansatz, and concluding with performance evaluation using specialized metrics against the AlphaFold baseline.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Sidechain packing is a critical stage in protein folding, with direct implications for structure-based drug discovery. Google DeepMind's tool, AlphaFold2, predicts protein backbone reliably but sidechain positioning less accurately. Recovering the lowest-energy rotamer assignment over a fixed backbone is NP-hard. We present a hybrid quantum-classical pipeline that repacks sidechains on the AlphaFold backbone using the Quantum Approximate Optimisation Algorithm (QAOA), encoding the one- and two-body energies as a quadratic unconstrained binary optimisation (QUBO) problem. We introduce a constraint-preserving ansatz, pairing a W-state initialisation with a cyclic XY ring mixer, that enforces one-hot rotamer validity without penalty terms while keeping two-qubit gate scaling linear in the rotamer count. We also define an asymptotic shot-scaling metric, measured against the experimentally resolved conformation, that fixes optimiser quality independently of the baseline; its fitted growth stays below the classical exhaustive-search rate at moderate rotamer flexibility. Evaluated on bovine pancreatic trypsin inhibitor (5PTI) across high- and moderate AlphaFold-confidence regions, the pipeline lowers conformational energy against the AlphaFold baseline.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.31223v1>

**Summary.** This paper explores the counterintuitive phenomenon of 'superactivation' in quantum interactive proof systems, showing that quantum communication can cause the cheating probability to increase dramatically upon parallel repetition. The authors prove that while the single-shot cheating value can be low, repeating the game multiple times forces the cheating probability to one. This work has significant implications for quantum complexity theory and the security analysis of quantum protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding that quantum resources can be 'superactivated' by repetition challenges standard assumptions about error amplification in quantum protocols, which is a core topic in quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how quantum communication affects the soundness error of interactive multiprover games (QMIP) under parallel repetition, contrasting this with classical results.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that quantum communication can cause 'superactivation' of cheating, where the cheating probability increases from a value less than one for a single instance to exactly one under parallel repetition.

**Method.** The work establishes a correspondence between quantum games and quantum channels, using techniques like teleportation-based reductions and analyzing the behavior of zero-error capacities.

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum interactive proof systems (QMIP) and quantum channels, analyzing the success probability of cheating provers (the 'value' of the game).

**Key observables.** The local value $\omega(G)$ versus the parallel repetition value $\omega(G^n)$, and the zero-error classical/quantum capacities ($C_0, Q_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of parallel repetitions ($n$), and the number of messages exchanged (limited to 3 for certain impossibility results).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The impossibility results for entangled values are shown only for a limited number of messages (at most 3), and the analysis relies on establishing specific orthogonality relations.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first contrasting classical and quantum parallel repetition effects, then introducing the quantum game-channel correspondence, proving the superactivation phenomenon for the local value, and finally proving the impossibility of superactivation for the entangled value under certain constraints.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study interactive multiprover games and many-round protocols in which the communication between the verifier and the provers is quantum. Parallel repetition is known to suppress soundness error of classical two-prover games arbitrarily close to zero, as shown by Raz (STOC '95), even with quantum strategies as shown by Yuen (ICALP '16), and Bavarian, Vidick and Yuen (STOC '17). Yet, we show that quantum communication can have the opposite, unexpected effect. Specifically, we exhibit a one-round quantum game and two-round $\mathsf{QMIP}$ protocols whose local (unentangled) value is strictly less than one for a single instance, yet equals one under $n$-fold parallel repetition for every $n \geq 2$. The key is that parallel repetition not only imposes additional winning conditions but also supplies additional quantum resources as entanglement in the exchanged states can be exploited jointly across copies.   Our multiround examples build on superactivation of zero-error capacities of quantum channels. To establish this connection, we introduce quantum games and interactive protocols which capture the one-shot zero-error classical and quantum capacities, both with and without entanglement assistance. Our constructions use quantum-state verification and a teleportation-based reduction from two rounds to one.   In contrast, when shared entanglement is allowed, we show that parallel repetition cannot increase the entangled value when at most $3$ messages are exchanged, consistent with classical results of Bellare, Impagliazzo and Naor (FOCS '97), and that of single-prover systems by Kitaev and Watrous (STOC '00). Combining this monotonicity with our quantum game-channel correspondence, we show that entanglement-assisted zero-error classical and quantum capacities cannot be superactivated.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a sophisticated information-theoretic measure to compare discrete probability distributions whose supports may not overlap. It achieves this by moving beyond comparing only the final discrete states, instead incorporating information about the continuous distributions and the specific discretization processes used to generate them. This provides a powerful tool for analyzing physical systems where the underlying continuous physics is more informative than the resulting coarse-grained data.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core topic is information geometry, the rigorous mathematical framework connecting extrinsic geometric operations (discretization) to intrinsic information metrics is highly relevant for developing robust comparison tools in complex many-body systems where approximations or coarse-graining are inevitable.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of comparing discrete probability distributions, particularly when their supports are non-inclusive, while retaining crucial information about the underlying continuous distributions and the discretization schemes used to generate them.

**Main result.** A novel representation-aware transport-information measure ($D_{RA}$) is introduced that successfully quantifies the difference between two discrete distributions by combining the divergence between their continuous references with a term quantifying the information deformation caused by their respective discretization processes.

**Method.** The measure is formulated using the framework of unavoidable canonical nonlinearity (UCN), linking the 2-Wasserstein transport cost of discretization to the information-geometric indistinguishability of nearby continuous distributions.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to general comparison of probability distributions, finding relevance in physical systems like configurational degrees of freedom (e.g., crystal structures) where the resulting discrete supports may be non-inclusive.

**Key observables.** The representation-aware transport-information measure ($D_{RA}$), the 2-Wasserstein-based transport cost ($	ext{eW}^2/2$), and the local information deformation terms ($	ext{Tr}(M_1A) + 	ext{Tr}(M_2C)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The continuous reference distributions ($P_{c1}, P_{c2}$), the discretization schemes ($D_1, D_2$), and the vanishing discretization scale limit ($	ext{V}_k ightarrow 0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes the local-map (LM) scheme for discretization and relies on the local UCN correspondence, which equates the discretization cost to the information-geometric indistinguishability at the leading order.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of standard information measures for non-inclusive supports. It then introduces the UCN framework to relate discretization geometry to information geometry. Finally, it derives and presents the concrete, second-order form of the representation-aware measure, separating the continuous difference from the discretization-induced deformation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Information-theoretic measures for comparing probability distributions are widely used across physics and other fields. When two discrete distributions have non-inclusive supports, however, the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence is in general not directly applicable, and various alternative divergences and distances have been introduced. These measures compare the resulting distributions themselves, but do not generally retain information about the representation transformations by which the discrete distributions are generated from underlying continuous ones. Here we introduce a representation-aware transport-information measure for discrete distributions with non-inclusive supports, formulated based on the standard KL divergence. We consider two continuous reference distributions, each transformed into a discrete representation through its own discretization scheme. Rather than comparing only the resulting discrete distributions or their continuous references, we additionally retain local information associated with the representation-change schemes. The resulting measure can therefore distinguish discrete representations that may have identical discrete probability landscapes but originate from different continuous references or discretization schemes. The construction is based on the transport-information cost of continuous-to-discrete representation in the framework of unavoidable canonical nonlinearity (UCN). UCN provides a non-arbitrary correspondence between the transport cost of discretization as an extrinsic geometric operation and the information-theoretic indistinguishability of nearby continuous distributions, thereby allowing a discrete representation to be associated with a local family of underlying continuous distributions on the statistical manifold.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a comprehensive mathematical characterization of the data-processing properties for a generalized family of quantum conditional Rényi entropies. By proving the sharpness of the data-processing region and deriving necessary conditions from universal monotonicity and entropic duality, the authors uniquely determine the structural constraints on these entropy measures. This work solidifies the mathematical understanding of information flow in quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous characterization of fundamental information-theoretic quantities like conditional entropy and their associated inequalities provides deep structural insights into quantum correlations, which underpins quantum computation theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to characterize the data-processing region, the club-sandwiched subfamily, and the entropic duality for a three-parameter family of quantum conditional Rényi entropies.

**Main result.** It proves that the data-processing region is sharp, that universal monotonicity under coarse-graining forces the parameter $z$ to equal $\alpha$, and that the entropic duality relations are rigid.

**Method.** The analysis uses rigorous mathematical proofs involving the application of specific test states (e.g., pure states, quantum-classical marginals) to derive necessary and sufficient conditions for various inequalities to hold.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the generalized quantum conditional Rényi entropy, $H_{\lambda}^{\alpha, z}(A|B)ho$, defined for bipartite quantum states $ho_{AB}$ in finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces.

**Key observables.** Quantum conditional Rényi entropies ($H_{\lambda}^{\alpha, z}$), the data-processing inequality (DPI), and the entropic duality relation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The entropy depends on three parameters: $\alpha > 0, \alpha 
eq 1$, $z > 0$, and $\lambda \in \mathbb{R}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces and relies on testing the functional against specific classes of pure and mixed states to constrain the parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the sharpness of the DPI region using various test states, then uses the condition of universal coarse-graining monotonicity to constrain the parameters, and finally uses the duality relation to uniquely characterize the entire structure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the three-parameter family of quantum conditional Rényi entropies $H_{α,z}^λ$ introduced in \textit{Commun. Math. Phys.} 407, 180 (2026). We prove that the previously established data-processing region is sharp. We then characterize the club-sandwiched family through a classical coarse-graining principle: within the full data-processing region, universal monotonicity under deterministic coarse-graining of the classical conditioned register holds if and only if $z = α$. Finally, we establish rigidity of entropic duality by showing that any universal duality relation for finite-dimensional tripartite pure states must satisfy the known parameter relations. Thus, the data-processing region, the club-sandwiched subfamily, and the duality transformation are each uniquely characterized by their respective universal properties.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper provides a precise mathematical criterion for determining if the entanglement of formation ($	ext{EF}$) for a two-qubit state possesses a global supporting affine functional. The existence hinges on the nondegeneracy of the state's support with respect to the spin-flip bilinear form. Failure of this condition leads to a characteristic square-root decay of $	ext{EF}$ under perturbation, demonstrating a fundamental limitation in the measure's global differentiability.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical characterization of when entanglement measures are well-behaved under small perturbations is crucial for developing robust quantum information protocols and understanding the boundaries of quantum correlations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence of a global supporting affine functional for the entanglement of formation ($	ext{EF}$) for an entangled two-qubit state.

**Main result.** A global supporting affine functional exists if and only if the spin-flip bilinear form restricted to the state's support is nondegenerate. In the degenerate case, $	ext{EF}$ decreases as the square root of the mixing parameter, violating the required bound.

**Method.** The analysis combines mathematical criteria involving the nondegeneracy of the spin-flip bilinear form with perturbation theory applied to the $	ext{EF}$ functional along specific paths.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on two-qubit quantum systems, characterized by density matrices $ho$. The mathematical tools involve the concurrence, the spin-flip bilinear form ($eta$), and the state's support ($S$).

**Key observables.** Entanglement of Formation ($	ext{EF}$), Concurrence ($C$), Spin-flip bilinear form ($eta$), and Concurrence roots ($\lambda_i$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degeneracy of $eta|_S$ (nondegenerate vs. rank two/three), and the small perturbation parameter $t$ or $\epsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the state $ho$ is entangled and relies on local Lipschitz continuity established for nondegenerate supports.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the provided notes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the mathematical criterion for the supporting functional's existence, analyzes the nondegenerate case where local Lipschitz continuity holds, and then investigates the degenerate case using perturbation analysis to show the failure of the global bound.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give a criterion for the existence of a global supporting affine functional for the entanglement of formation at an entangled two-qubit state. It exists if and only if the spin-flip bilinear form is nondegenerate on the support of the state, or equivalently, if the last concurrence root associated with the support is nonzero. In this case the entanglement of formation is locally Lipschitz continuous. In the degenerate case the state has rank two or three, and its projective support is tangent to the product-state quadric. For suitable perturbations outside the support, the entanglement of formation decreases as the square root of the mixing parameter. The leading term is obtained explicitly. This square-root decrease violates the one-sided Lipschitz bound required for a global supporting affine functional.

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally investigates how the magnetic anisotropy and Crystal-Electric-Field (CEF) ground states evolve across the $	ext{SmTr}_2	ext{Ge}_2$ series by varying the transition metal $	ext{Tr}$. By measuring magnetization, specific heat, and susceptibility, the authors demonstrate a clear transition in magnetic behavior linked to the underlying CEF state and the local structural environment around the $	ext{Sm}$ ion.

**Why it may be interesting.** The systematic tuning of magnetic anisotropy via ligand environment (changing $	ext{Tr}$) provides a clear experimental platform to study how crystal fields dictate the low-energy magnetic excitations in $4f$ electron systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To systematically investigate the evolution of magnetic anisotropy and crystal-electric-field (CEF) ground states in the $	ext{SmTr}_2	ext{Ge}_2$ series.

**Main result.** The magnetic anisotropy systematically changes from easy $ab$ plane ($	ext{Co}, 	ext{Ni}$) to easy $c$ axis ($	ext{Cu}, 	ext{Ag}$), correlating with the evolution of the CEF ground state and local structural parameters.

**Method.** Measurements include magnetization, specific heat, and magnetic susceptibility ($\chi$) as a function of temperature and applied field, complemented by structural analysis.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the $	ext{SmTr}_2	ext{Ge}_2$ compounds ($	ext{Tr} = 	ext{Co}, 	ext{Ni}, 	ext{Cu}, 	ext{Ag}$) with the $	ext{ThCr}_2	ext{Si}_2$-type structure, where magnetism originates from the $	ext{Sm}^{3+}$ $4f$ electrons.

**Key observables.** Magnetic anisotropy (easy plane vs. easy axis), CEF ground state evolution, magnetic entropy, and resistivity ($ho(T)$) changes below $T_N$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transition metal identity ($	ext{Tr}$), applied magnetic field ($H=1	ext{ T}$), and temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses $	ext{La}$ nonmagnetic analogs to isolate the $	ext{Sm}$ contribution to magnetic susceptibility and specific heat.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $ho(T)$ vs $T$ for various $	ext{Tr}$ elements, $\chi(T)$ vs $T$ for $H \parallel a$ and $H \parallel c$, and $C_p$ vs $T$ data used to track magnetic entropy evolution.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes magnetic measurements ($\chi, C_p, M$) across the $	ext{Tr}$ series, correlating the observed changes in magnetic anisotropy and CEF ground state with the local structural environment determined by X-ray measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigated the crystal-electric-field (CEF) states in SmTr2Ge2 (Tr= Co-Cu and Ag) with the ThCr2Si2-type structure by magnetization and specific heat measurements. The magnetic anisotropy evolves systematically with Tr, changing from an easy ab plane for Tr = Co and Ni to an easy c axis for Tr = Cu and Ag. Combined analyses of the low-temperature susceptibility and magnetic entropy indicate a level crossing of the CEF ground state between Ni and Cu, together with a systematic evolution of the first excited CEF energy. The evolution of the magnetic properties correlates with changes in local structural parameters, providing an experimental basis for discussing how the ligand environment around the Sm ion is related to the 4f electronic state.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a general Non-Markovian Electronic Friction and Langevin Dynamics framework to simulate molecular dynamics coupled to metal leads. It demonstrates that retaining non-Markovian electronic memory effects is essential for accurately describing non-equilibrium charge transport and vibrational energy exchange. The method successfully reproduces exact quantum benchmarks where simpler Markovian approximations fail.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles open quantum systems dynamics by providing a rigorous, non-perturbative method to incorporate electronic memory effects into vibrational dynamics, which is crucial for understanding energy transfer in nanoscale devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of accurately simulating the non-equilibrium vibronic dynamics of molecules coupled to metal surfaces, particularly when electronic memory effects are significant.

**Main result.** The proposed Non-Markovian Electronic Friction and Langevin Dynamics (NM-EFLD) framework is shown to be significantly more accurate and robust than the conventional Markovian approximation, especially under finite bias conditions.

**Method.** The authors develop a general framework utilizing a Markovian embedding scheme to sample coordinate-dependent quantum colored noise, allowing the simulation of non-Markovian electronic friction effects.

**Model / system.** The model involves a molecule coupled to metal leads, described by a Hamiltonian decomposed into molecular, metal, and interaction terms. The demonstration uses a vibrationally coupled donor-acceptor nanojunction.

**Key observables.** Nonequilibrium charge transport, steady-state vibrational excitation ($\langle N_{vib} angle_{ss}$), and the comparison of energy dissipation rates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bias voltage ($\Phi$), temperature ($T$), and the relative importance of non-Markovian vs. Markovian electronic memory effects.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the Markovian approximation fails in non-equilibrium regimes where electronic memory is crucial; the method overcomes this by systematically decomposing memory kernels.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between NM-EFLD and HEOM benchmarks, showing the failure of the Markovian approach at high bias, and visualizing the dependence of friction and noise on vibrational coordinate and voltage.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the general Hamiltonian and the need for non-Markovian treatments, detailing the NM-EFLD equations, and finally presenting numerical results comparing the full non-Markovian approach against the Markovian limit and exact quantum benchmarks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a general framework for simulating the nonequilibrium vibronic dynamics of molecules interacting with metal surfaces, which utilizes non-Markovian electronic friction and the corresponding generalized Langevin equation. The method employs a Markovian embedding scheme to sample coordinate-dependent quantum colored noise, with the underlying memory kernels represented systematically by exponential decompositions obtained directly from the electronic forces. In doing so, the approach extends the widely used electronic friction and Langevin dynamics formalism beyond the conventional Markovian approximation while retaining the coordinate dependence of the electronic back-action. We demonstrate the method for nonequilibrium charge transport through a vibrationally coupled molecular junction, where comparisons with numerically exact hierarchical equations of motion simulations show that the non-Markovian formulation is both more accurate and more robust than its Markovian counterpart. In particular, we find that non-Markovian electronic forces play a decisive role in the nonequilibrium vibrational dynamics, stabilizing regimes in which the conventional Markovian approximation fails.

</details>

<sub>Figure 5(a) shows the A = 1 mW DS endpoint statis- tics. The seed displaces and broadens the crossover threshold. Thus, the weak seeding signal broadens the DSR domain. The dependence of the multipulse genera- tion probability on the C-parameter is weak. Phase jitter slightly worsens DSR stability, but time jitter improves it.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes pulse selection in a laser system operating near a dissipative-soliton-resonance regime using a stochastic Ginzburg-Landau model. The key finding is that injecting a weak optical seed actively suppresses the formation of multiple pulses, thereby stabilizing the single-pulse state and broadening the operational energy window. The methodology relies heavily on advanced numerical simulations and careful statistical analysis to distinguish true resonance effects from controlled stabilization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges nonlinear optics, stochastic processes, and non-equilibrium statistical mechanics by showing how external coherent control can stabilize complex, dissipative states against inherent noise-driven instabilities.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how weak optical seeding controls the competition between single-pulse operation and noise-induced multipulsing in a laser operating in the dissipative-soliton-resonance (DSR) regime.

**Main result.** Weak optical seeding suppresses the tendency toward multipulsing, thereby enhancing the energy scalability of dissipative solitons and the DSR window. This control mechanism is distinct from canonical stochastic resonance.

**Method.** The research employs numerical simulations solving the stochastic cubic–quintic complex Ginzburg–Landau (CGLE) equation, analyzing endpoint statistics across various noise and energy parameter grids.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by a stochastic CGLE equation, representing a weakly seeded, normal-dispersion laser. The analysis is applied to a physical realization using a Cr2+:ZnS Cavity Photonic Oscillator (CPO).

**Key observables.** Pulse number (single vs. multi-pulse), DSR energy window, relative probability of multipulsing (P2), and coherence-sensitive diagnostics like the directional slope (Theta_s).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dissipative-soliton-resonance (DSR) regime, weak optical seed strength, and noise levels (modeled via Gaussian white noise or phase diffusion).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors distinguish between stationary DS existence, seeded DS robustness, and the accessible DSR range; they caution against equating stationary energy windows with resonance evidence.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference aggregate probability plots (Fig. 6) showing endpoint classifications, and plots detailing the relationship between spectral coordinates (r) and energy (E') (Fig. 2).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the stochastic model (CGLE), then detailing the simulation methods (noise sources, scans), presenting the core findings regarding seeding control, and concluding with a careful discussion distinguishing their results from established resonance phenomena.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study pulse-number selection in a stochastic cubic--quintic complex Ginzburg--Landau model of a weakly seeded, normal-dispersion laser in the dissipative-soliton-resonance (DSR) regime. Optical seed injection suppresses energy-dependent multipulsing and produces a nonmonotonic DSR energy window. The noise-induced formation statistics do not establish canonical stochastic resonance or escape from a pre-existing soliton. Deterministic control by a weak monochromatic seed yields persistent single-pulse behavior within a noticeably broader energy window, enhancing dissipative-soliton energy scalability and DSR.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses microwave spectroscopy to characterize Josephson Junction Arrays (JJAs) made from Al/InAs heterostructures. By measuring the plasmonic resonance frequency as a function of an out-of-plane magnetic field, the authors extract the Josephson inductance and the current-phase relation. This demonstrates the utility of JJAs as tunable testbeds for studying superconducting phenomena in hybrid quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a direct experimental platform to probe the microscopic superconducting properties (like Andreev states) in semiconductor-superconductor interfaces using macroscopic circuit measurements (plasmon resonance), which is highly relevant for superconducting quantum circuit development.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to characterize the Josephson inductance and study the plasmonic modes of Josephson Junction Arrays (JJAs) fabricated on hybrid superconductor-semiconductor heterostructures.

**Main result.** The measurements successfully demonstrated the tunability of the Josephson inductance over a broad range of out-of-plane magnetic fields, allowing for the extraction of the current-phase relation (CPR) and effective transparency.

**Method.** Microwave characterization measurements were performed by probing the low-energy plasmonic modes of the JJA, analyzing the resulting Fraunhofer-like diffraction patterns.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a JJA built on an Al/InAs heterostructure, configured as the center conductor of a coplanar waveguide resonator. The analysis relies on fitting the magnetic field dependence of the resonance frequency to extract the Josephson inductance and CPR.

**Key observables.** Josephson inductance ($L_J$), plasmonic modes dispersion relation $\omega(k)$, and the Fraunhofer-like diffraction pattern of the resonance frequency.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Out-of-plane magnetic field ($B_{\perp}$), temperature ($T \approx 20 - 100 	ext{ mK}$), and the superconducting gap parameter ($\Delta^*N \approx 16 	ext{ meV}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes $C_0/C_J = 2/3$ for dispersion relation calculations, and background subtraction is performed using reference measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the device layout and circuit model, the plasmon dispersion relation, and microwave transmission traces illustrating the interference patterns (Fraunhofer patterns) for different devices at various magnetic fields.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure of presenting the physical setup (JJA on Al/InAs), detailing the microwave measurement technique (probing plasmon modes), and then analyzing the magnetic field dependence of the resonance to extract fundamental superconducting parameters like $L_J$ and the CPR.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present microwave characterization measurements of Josephson junction arrays (JJAs) based on epitaxial Al-InAs quantum well heterostructure. The Josephson inductance of the constituent planar junctions can be derived by probing the low-energy plasmon modes of these devices. Applying an out-of-plane magnetic field gives rise to a Fraunhofer-like diffraction pattern, from which the current-phase relation and the effective transparency can be extracted. JJAs can be used to achieve high inductances, suitable for the implementation of quantum circuits. They also provide an excellent test-bed for studying the microscopic excitations of hybrid superconductor-semiconductor devices associated with the presence of Andreev states. Here, we demonstrate their tunability over a broad range of out-of-plane magnetic fields.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a theoretical framework showing how strain and magnetic fields can control the topology of electronic states at a crystal-field crossing in Ce3+. By analyzing the effective Hamiltonian, the authors predict the existence of a topologically non-trivial point (Chern charge = 1) that can be accessed by tuning strain and magnetic field, offering a route for quantum control in rare-earth materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, symmetry-dictated pathway to engineer topological quantum states (diabolical points) using external, controllable parameters like mechanical strain, which is highly relevant for quantum control in solid-state systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a symmetry-based framework connecting high-field crystal-field reconstruction, strain response, and parameter-space topology in rare-earth systems.

**Main result.** The combination of strain and field detuning can generate an isolated diabolical point with unit-magnitude Chern charge in the 3D control space of a cubic Ce3+ ion.

**Method.** Theoretical derivation using an effective two-level Hamiltonian, analyzing the topological features (Chern charge) generated by independent strain and magnetic field controls.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using the cubic Ce3+ ion ($4f^1$) crystal-field splitting, governed by an effective Hamiltonian that incorporates strain ($\epsilon_{xz}, \epsilon_{yz}$) and magnetic field detuning ($\delta B$).

**Key observables.** Chern charge ($|C|=1$), gap opening ($\propto |\epsilon_{\perp}|$), elastic susceptibility ($\delta c_{44}$), and geometric phase ($\gamma_{\pm}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ideal crossing field near $35.5 	ext{ T}$ (for CeTe); representative shear coupling $|\alpha_{T2g}| \simeq 18.1 	ext{ meV}$ per unit tensor strain.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a single-ion point-charge model, which neglects full hybridization effects in the actual compound.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spectrum at the CeTe scale, the gap opening due to strain, and the Bloch-sphere trajectory used to calculate the Berry phase for quantum control.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the symmetry-protected crossing, deriving the effective Hamiltonian, calculating the topological invariant (Chern charge) using strain and field detuning as controls, and finally discussing the experimental signatures (elastic/magnetic).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Field-tuned crystal-field crossings provide a route to synthetic topology in localized rare-earth degrees of freedom when symmetry-resolved perturbations can independently control the crossing states. Here we show that a field-induced crossing in cubic Ce$^{3+}$ has precisely this structure. For $B\parallel[001]$, the crossing is protected by a twofold rotation, while the $T_{2g}$ shears $ε_{xz}$ and $ε_{yz}$ break that protection and mix the two states through orthogonal pseudospin components. Together with magnetic-field detuning, these controls generate an isolated diabolical point with unit-magnitude Chern charge in a three-dimensional parameter space. Using the CeTe crystal-field scale gives an ideal single-ion crossing near $35.5$ T. A calibrated point-charge calculation gives a representative projected shear coupling $|α_{T_{2g}}|\simeq18.1$ meV per unit tensor strain, corresponding to gaps of $0.036$ and $0.072$ meV for tensor shears of $0.10\%$ and $0.20\%$. We identify the associated elastic and magnetic signatures, discuss the limitations of the electrostatic estimate in a hybridizing Ce compound, and determine the adiabatic and coherence requirements for cyclic geometric control. The result is a symmetry-based framework that connects high-field crystal-field reconstruction, strain response, and parameter-space topology in rare-earth systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study explores topological superconductivity in an altermagnet-superconductor heterostructure, focusing on the role of strain and hopping anisotropy. By calculating the Chern number, the authors demonstrate that structural distortions can induce robust topological phases. This work suggests that strain offers a powerful, controllable route to realizing topological superconductivity in magnetic heterostructures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between lattice strain (a structural parameter) and the realization of exotic topological superconducting states is highly relevant for designing novel quantum materials and superconducting devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the emergence and control of topological superconductivity within a heterostructure composed of an s-wave superconductor and a d-wave altermagnet.

**Main result.** Strain-induced lattice distortion and hopping anisotropy are shown to be effective mechanisms that allow topological superconducting phases with high Chern numbers (up to |C|=2) to emerge over broad parameter regions.

**Method.** The authors calculate the Chern number using the Bogoliubov-de Gennes Hamiltonian and confirm the topological nature by observing gapless Majorana edge modes under open boundary conditions.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D heterostructure combining an s-wave superconductor and a d-wave altermagnet, modeled using the Bogoliubov–de Gennes Hamiltonian. The model incorporates strain effects via a parameter 'b' and Rashba spin-orbit coupling.

**Key observables.** Topological phase diagrams characterized by the Chern number (C), and the existence of gapless Majorana edge modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strain parameter (b), chemical potential ($\mu$), and hopping anisotropy ($t_x$ vs $t_y$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes fixed parameters for the superconducting gap ($\Delta_s$) and spin-orbit coupling ($\alpha$), and simplifies gap closing analysis by setting $\sin k_x = \sin k_y = 0$ at high-symmetry points.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the heterostructure, the momentum dependence of the effective magnetic field under strain, the resulting topological phase diagrams showing |C| regions, and energy spectra confirming gapless Majorana edge modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the model Hamiltonian, calculating the Chern number to map out topological phase diagrams as a function of strain and anisotropy, and finally confirming the physical realization of the topological phases via the detection of Majorana edge modes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate topological superconductivity in a heterostructure consisting of a two-dimensional $s$-wave superconductor and a $d$-wave altermagnet with Rashba spin-orbit coupling. In particular, we study the effects of strain and hopping anisotropy on the topological superconducting phases. By calculating the Chern number, we obtain topological phase diagrams as functions of the chemical potential and the strength of the effective magnetic field induced by the magnetic proximity effect. We find that strain-induced lattice distortion allows topological superconducting phases to emerge over a broad parameter region. Our findings suggest that strain that lowers symmetry can serve as a route to realizing topological superconductivity in altermagnet-superconductor heterostructures.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a quantitative phase-field model to accurately simulate hydrogen diffusion and trapping in polycrystalline metals, explicitly accounting for the interconnected nature of grain boundaries. It corrects deficiencies in older models by treating GBs as continuous pathways rather than isolated traps. The results show that grain boundary transport fundamentally alters measurable properties like TDS spectra and effective diffusion coefficients.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on materials science, the rigorous development of a coupled, multi-scale transport model involving equilibrium constraints and anisotropic diffusion echoes complex non-equilibrium dynamics found in open quantum systems or reaction-diffusion processes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the limitations of conventional hydrogen trapping models that fail to account for the interconnected nature of grain boundaries (GBs) in polycrystalline metals.

**Main result.** The quantitative phase-field model demonstrates that grain boundary diffusion significantly shifts TDS peaks, invalidating simple Kissinger-type analyses, and shows effective diffusion coefficients depend strongly on multiple material parameters.

**Method.** A quantitative phase-field model is developed to simulate coupled hydrogen diffusion, trapping, and transport along grain boundaries.

**Model / system.** The system is hydrogen diffusing and trapping in polycrystalline metals with a static microstructure. The model uses phase-field methods to describe the microstructure and solves coupled differential equations for hydrogen site fractions in both the bulk lattice and the GB regions.

**Key observables.** Total trapped hydrogen content, hydrogen fluxes (parallel/perpendicular to GBs), effective diffusion coefficients ($D_{eff,ss}$), and TDS peak shifts.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Grain size, trapping free energy ($\Delta\mu_{H,b,GB}$), temperature, and grain boundary mobility.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The microstructure is assumed static (no grain movement), and the model neglects mechanical effects like stress-assisted diffusion.

**Figures summary.** Figure 12 shows the relative error in total hydrogen concentration over time for a perpendicular flux benchmark. Figure 13 compares simulated effective steady-state diffusion coefficients against an analytical fit.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the quantitative phase-field model framework, then applies it to benchmark simulations (e.g., perpendicular flux) to validate the methodology, and finally applies it to interpret experimental observables like TDS spectra and effective diffusion coefficients.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Grain boundaries influence hydrogen transport in polycrystalline metals by acting as both trapping sites and interconnected diffusion pathways. Conventional interpretations of thermal desorption spectroscopy (TDS) and permeation experiments often treat traps as isolated defects, neglecting grain boundary connectivity and potentially misinterpreting experimental data. Here, we develop a quantitative phase-field model for hydrogen diffusion, grain boundary trapping, and grain boundary-assisted transport. The formulation preserves the physical grain boundary volume independently of the numerical interface thickness and accounts for anisotropic diffusion parallel and perpendicular to grain boundaries. Benchmark simulations verify quantitative behavior under interface upscaling. The model shows that grain boundary diffusion can significantly shift TDS peaks, making Kissinger-type analyses unreliable when grain boundary transport is significant. Effective diffusion coefficients are also shown to depend strongly on grain size, trapping free energy, temperature, and grain boundary mobility. These results highlight the coupled role of trapping thermodynamics, bulk diffusion, and grain-boundary transport.

</details>

<sub>Figure 3d compares the simulated reduced total scattering structure functions across the three approaches using the best-fitting MD-sampled NP. To this comparison, we make two remarks. First, the idealized structure results in sharper features than the more disordered relaxed and MD-sampled structures with modified strain. Second, the static methods fail to capture the decay of F(Q) at high Q; the MD-sampled structure exhibits a more realistic decay and a flatter residual than the two static methods. Thus, temperature effects are not properly captured with a Debye-Waller correction. Direct sampling of the PES through MD is more accurate, although computationally more expensive.</sub>

**Summary.** This work introduces an energetically driven framework to analyze total X-ray scattering data from nanoparticles in real-time for autonomous experiments. By comparing idealized, MLIP-relaxed, and MD-sampled structures, the authors demonstrate that advanced sampling methods are crucial for accurately determining particle morphology and size. This provides a pathway for integrating computational structure refinement directly into closed-loop experimental campaigns.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on materials science, the core methodology—developing robust, computationally efficient simulation pipelines (MLIPs, MD sampling) to interpret complex, noisy experimental data—is highly relevant to developing simulation tools for quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a real-time, energetically driven framework to convert total X-ray scattering data into reliable, atomistic structural models for autonomous materials discovery.

**Main result.** A hierarchical workflow combining rapid idealized screening with targeted MLIP relaxation and MD refinement significantly improves structural feedback compared to idealized models, leading to better size and morphology determination.

**Method.** The authors compare three structural sampling methods—idealized, MLIP-relaxed, and MD-sampled—to fit experimental total X-ray scattering data using a weighted profile residual ($R_{wp}$).

**Model / system.** The study uses gold nanoparticles (NPs) with various crystal morphologies (spherical, octahedral, decahedral, icosahedral) whose structures are modeled using MLIPs and sampled via MD simulations.

**Key observables.** Total X-ray scattering data, weighted profile residual ($R_{wp}$), particle size, and internal strain ($\epsilon_i$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (e.g., 300 K, 250 K), scattering momentum transfer ($Q$), and the tolerance for size estimation ($\Delta R_{wp} = 0.01$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Idealized models neglect surface relaxation, strain, and thermal disorder; MD simulations often approximate explicit water solvation for computational feasibility.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare scattering results across idealized, relaxed, and MD-sampled geometries, showing that MD sampling yields the best fit and size estimates. Other figures detail convergence, strain analysis, and computational speed-ups.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the need for real-time analysis in autonomous labs, then comparing the accuracy of idealized vs. MLIP/MD sampling on gold NPs, and finally detailing the hierarchical workflow for iterative structure refinement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The emergence of autonomous laboratories motivates rapid conversion of experimental data into reliable atomistic models on time-scales compatible with closed-loop optimization. Here we develop an energetically driven structure matching framework for analysis during ongoing total X-ray scattering experiments. Using data from gold nanoparticles, we match against idealized spherical, octahedral, decahedral, and icosahedral geometries, their machine-learned interatomic potential (MLIP)-relaxed structures, and molecular dynamics (MD) ensembles. Idealized models are fast to generate but can misassign morphology and systematically underestimate size by neglecting surface relaxation, strain, and thermal disorder. MLIP relaxation markedly improves both, while MD ensemble averaging agrees best with experiment. We therefore introduce a hierarchical workflow combining rapid idealized screening with targeted MLIP and MD refinement of top candidates, delivering improved structural feedback without interrupting autonomous operation. This framework provides a route towards autonomous campaigns in which the target structure itself can be updated in response to the evolving energy landscape of structures compatible with the experimental data.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the Faraday and Kerr effects in lattices of conducting disks, proposing that the rotation arises from a nonlinear, pump-induced Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$). The theory predicts significant enhancement of these optical responses when the pump and probe frequencies approach the plasmon resonance, successfully explaining large rotation angles observed experimentally.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mechanism relies on nonlinear optical response ($\sigma_{xy} \propto |E|^2$) rather than the standard Inverse Faraday Effect, providing a novel theoretical understanding of giant optical responses in structured materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the mechanism and magnitude of pump-induced Faraday and Kerr rotation in a two-dimensional lattice of conducting disks.

**Main result.** The rotation is shown to originate from a third-order nonlinear response inducing a Hall-like conductivity ($\sigma_{xy} = -\sigma_{yx}$), leading to giant enhancement near plasmon resonance, matching experimental observations.

**Method.** The analysis uses a theoretical framework involving solving Maxwell's equations and calculating the nonlinear conductivity tensor ($\sigma_{xy}$) using time-dependent perturbation theory.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a square lattice of conducting disks (oblate spheroids) embedded in a dielectric medium, analyzed using a quasi-two-dimensional approximation.

**Key observables.** Faraday and Kerr rotation angles ($	heta_F, 	heta_K$) and ellipticities ($\epsilon_F, \epsilon_K$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Plasmon resonance frequency ($\omega_0$), pump intensity ($1 	ext{ kW/cm}^2$), lattice period ($a$), and dielectric constants ($\epsilon_1, \epsilon_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the low-intensity regime ($|\sigma_{xy}| \ll |\sigma_{xx}|$) and utilizes approximations for the current density and field components.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the photoinduced Faraday/Kerr effects, showing resonances in $	heta_F$ and $\epsilon_F$ vs. probe frequency ($\omega$) when the pump frequency ($\Omega$) is tuned to the plasmon resonance ($\omega_0$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by deriving the nonlinear $\sigma_{xy}$ from the electron gas response, then calculates the resulting Faraday/Kerr response using transmission/reflection coefficients, and finally analyzes the resonance enhancement near the plasmon frequency.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a theoretical study of pump-induced Faraday and Kerr rotation in a two-dimensional lattice of conducting disks. We propose a mechanism of Faraday and Kerr responses in which a circularly polarized pump directly induces a high-frequency Hall component $σ_{xy}=-σ_{yx}$ of the electron conductivity tensor at the probe frequency. Microscopically, $σ_{xy}$ originates from the third-order nonlinear response of the electron gas rather than from the real magnetic field created by solenoidal charge currents in the inverse Faraday effect. We show that the Faraday and Kerr rotation angles are significantly enhanced when the pump and probe frequencies are tuned close to the plasmon resonance of the disks, reaching $\sim 0.1^\circ$ per 1~kW/cm$^2$ of incident pump intensity in the terahertz range. This mechanism can explain recently observed giant pump-induced Faraday rotation in graphene disk lattices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically investigates how nonlinear carrier dynamics govern the spin-orbit torque (SOT) in TMDC/CrI3 heterostructures. It establishes that symmetry forbids linear spin responses, making nonlinear intra-band transitions the dominant mechanism. The findings highlight that the resulting SOT is highly tunable by external fields and material choice, positioning these bilayers as key platforms for low-power spintronics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter spintronics, the detailed analysis of non-equilibrium carrier dynamics and symmetry-protected nonlinear responses provides theoretical tools applicable to understanding current-driven phenomena in quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the role of nonlinear carrier dynamics in generating spin-orbit torque (SOT) within van der Waals magnetic heterostructures.

**Main result.** The SOT is dominated by nonlinear intra-band transitions, generating a purely field-like torque whose magnitude and sign are highly tunable by doping, twist angle, and gate electric field.

**Method.** The analysis employs a single-band steady-state Boltzmann approach, focusing on nonlinear transport beyond the linear-response regime.

**Model / system.** The system is the TMDC/CrI3 bilayer (e.g., WSe2/CrI3, MoSe2/CrI3), where the TMDC layer interacts with the 2D ferromagnet CrI3 via proximity exchange.

**Key observables.** Spin polarization, Spin-Orbit Torque (SOT), and the dependence of SOT on chemical potential and doping type.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Zero-Rashba SOC limit, chemical potential ($\mu$), doping type ($n$- vs $p$-doped), twist angle, and gate electric field.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The linear spin polarization is forbidden by symmetry, necessitating the focus on nonlinear intraband transitions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the chemical potential dependence of non-zero spin polarization components and the resulting SOT for both MoSe2/CrI3 and WSe2/CrI3 bilayers under varying magnetization directions.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a theoretical model for nonlinear SOT, presents numerical results for non-equilibrium spin polarization, and concludes by summarizing the findings regarding the tunability of the SOT.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The role of nonlinear carrier dynamics in current-driven spin phenomena remains poorly understood in van der Waals magnetic heterostructures. Here, we investigate the spin polarization and the resulting spin-orbit torque (SOT) in transition-metal dichalcogenide/chromium iodide (TMDC/CrI$_3$) heterostructures, focusing on WSe$_2$/CrI$_3$ and MoSe$_2$/CrI$_3$, in the zero-Rashba spin-orbit coupling (SOC) limit and beyond the linear-response regime. We find that linear spin polarization is forbidden by symmetry, making nonlinear intraband transitions the dominant mechanism in this regime. Consequently, the current-driven spin response differs fundamentally from conventional linear-response predictions. The resulting nonlinear spin polarization generates a purely field-like torque whose magnitude and sign depend sensitively on the chemical potential and doping type, with pronounced asymmetries between n- and p-doped systems. A strong material dependence is also observed: n-doped MoSe$_2$/CrI$_3$ exhibits a torque nearly an order of magnitude larger than that of WSe$_2$/CrI$_3$, together with two sign reversals, reflecting differences in their SOC and proximity-exchange parameters. Structural and electrostatic control through the twist angle and gate electric field provides additional means of tuning the nonlinear torque, including substantial modulation and controllable sign reversals. These findings establish TMDC/CrI$_3$ bilayers as a versatile platform for engineering nonlinear spin-orbit phenomena in ultrathin, low-power spintronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes using non-uniform quantum well and barrier thickness engineering in HfO2-based ferroelectric tunnel junctions to solve the trade-off between high TER and low RA. By coupling a ferroelectric model with NEGF, the authors demonstrate that precise structural control can align resonant states to achieve record performance metrics for next-generation non-volatile memory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed quantum transport modeling of resonant tunneling in ferroelectrics provides insights into how engineered nanoscale structures can control electronic properties, which is relevant to understanding open quantum systems dynamics in solid-state devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is simultaneously achieving a high Tunneling Electro-Resistance Ratio (TER) and a low Resistance-Area (RA) product in HfO2-based ferroelectric tunnel junctions (FTJs).

**Main result.** Non-uniform quantum well (QW) and barrier thickness engineering is shown to be a robust design parameter that controls resonant-state alignment, leading to high TER (up to 1x10^8%) and low LRS RA (as low as 1 $\Omega\cdot\text{cm}^2$).

**Method.** The study employs a self-consistent Non-Equilibrium Green's Function (NEGF) formalism coupled with a Preisach-based ferroelectric (FE) model.

**Model / system.** The system is an HfO2-based multi-QW FTJ structure. The model integrates the FE polarization state (via Preisach model) with quantum transport calculations (via NEGF) to determine current density.

**Key observables.** Tunneling Electro-Resistance Ratio (TER), Resistance-Area (RA) product, and resonant transmission characteristics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Non-uniform QW and barrier thicknesses ($t_{	ext{qw1}}, t_{	ext{qw2}}$, etc.), and the inclusion of self-consistent elastic scattering terms.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that transport changes are primarily due to thickness-dependent resonant tunneling rather than major changes in the ferroelectric polarization state.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the FTJ architectures, the simulation framework, and show how varying QW/IL thicknesses and incorporating elastic scattering positively influence the TER and RA performance.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates the impact of QW/barrier non-uniformity, first establishing the baseline using NEGF/Preisach models, then optimizing the structure by tuning thicknesses, and finally demonstrating the positive influence of elastic scattering on the resulting high-performance junction.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

HfO$_2$-based ferroelectric tunnel junctions (FTJs) are promising candidates for scalable non-volatile memory, but simultaneously achieving a high tunneling electro-resistance ratio (TER) and a low resistance-area (RA) product remains challenging. To address this challenge, this work introduces non-uniform quantum well (QW) and barrier thickness engineering in HfO$_2$-based multi-QW FTJs using a self-consistent Preisach-based ferroelectric (FE) model integrated with the coherent and inelastic non-equilibrium Green's function (NEGF) formalism. The non-uniform well and barrier configuration produces a wide range of FTJ design landscapes due to closely spaced, broad resonant states in the low resistance state (LRS) and a larger separation in the high resistance state (HRS), resulting in strong polarization-dependent resonant transmission with TER reaching the order of $\mathbf{1\times10^{8}\%}$ and an LRS RA product as low as $\mathbf{1~Ω\cdot\mathrm{cm}^{2}}$ at read bias and in the presence of scattering. By incorporating self-consistent elastic scattering into the NEGF framework, we also show that elastic scattering can positively influence the TER and RA performance of non-uniform FTJs by progressively increasing the overlap of the closely spaced resonances. Overall, the results establish non-uniform QW and barrier thickness as an effective and robust design parameter for controlling resonant-state alignment and achieving a favorable combination of high TER and low RA in HfO$_2$-based multi-QW FTJs.

</details>
