**Summary.** This work investigates superconductivity in NbReSi, finding that while the pairing is phonon-mediated, standard theoretical models overestimate the critical temperature. The authors successfully reconcile theory with experiment by rigorously including anharmonic lattice effects and electron-phonon vertex corrections, establishing a quantitative understanding of the pairing mechanism.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the rigorous application of advanced many-body perturbation theory (Eliashberg equations) to model emergent quantum phenomena like superconductivity is relevant to understanding strongly coupled quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the microscopic origin of superconductivity in the noncentrosymmetric material NbReSi and to accurately calculate its critical temperature ($T_c$).

**Main result.** The superconductivity is established as phonon-mediated, but quantitative agreement with experiment requires going beyond harmonic Migdal-Eliashberg theory by including anharmonic lattice dynamics and vertex corrections.

**Method.** The study uses first-principles calculations (DFT/DFPT) to solve the isotropic Eliashberg equations, incorporating anharmonic phonon renormalization and lowest-order electron-phonon vertex corrections.

**Model / system.** The system is the noncentrosymmetric superconductor NbReSi. The theory models electron-phonon coupling ($\lambda$) and the superconducting gap ($\Delta$) using advanced many-body techniques.

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), superconducting gap ($\Delta(0)$), electron-phonon coupling constant ($\lambda$), and phonon frequencies ($\omega$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The necessity of anharmonicity and vertex corrections to match the experimental $T_c$ of 6.5 K.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial assumption of conventional phonon-mediated pairing is tested against advanced theoretical limits (harmonic vs. anharmonic, with/without vertex corrections).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate electronic structure, phonon dispersion (comparing harmonic and anharmonic treatments), and the Eliashberg spectral function $\alpha^2F(\omega)$ under various theoretical approximations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the electronic structure, then calculating the electron-phonon coupling using harmonic theory, demonstrating its overestimation of $T_c$, and finally refining the calculation by incorporating anharmonicity and vertex corrections to match experimental results.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The noncentrosymmetric superconductor NbReSi (Tc=6.5 K) is being actively explored for unconventional pairing that is allowed by its broken inversion symmetry. Despite broad experimental interest, what drives its superconductivity remains an open question. We show that the electronic states at the Fermi level are dominated by Nb and Re $d$-states. Vibrations of the same heavy-metal framework contribute more than 95% of the electron-phonon coupling, with the strongest coupling arising from the sublattice-selective breathing mode involving strongly bonded Re atoms. Harmonic Migdal-Eliashberg theory overestimates Tc by more than 60%. The zero-point anharmonic hardening of these modes reduces the coupling by 20%. The leading vertex correction becomes important as well despite low phonon energies due to the contribution of spatially localized Re-d orbitals to the electronic band structure near the Fermi level. Together, anharmonicity and vertex corrections bring the calculated Tc and superconducting gap into close agreement with experiment, establishing NbReSi as a moderately coupled, phonon-mediated superconductor whose quantitative description requires going beyond harmonic Migdal--Eliashberg theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes how surface plasmons enhance the weak quadrupole transitions of Rydberg excitons confined within core-shell nanostructures. By modeling the near-field enhancement and incorporating the large size of Rydberg excitons, the authors predict optimal structural dimensions that maximize this quantum optical effect.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges quantum optics (Rydberg atoms/excitons) with nanophotonics (plasmons), providing a quantitative framework for engineering light-matter interactions to enhance otherwise weak, forbidden transitions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the enhancement of quadrupole transitions in Rydberg excitons due to their interaction with surface plasmons within core-shell nanostructures.

**Main result.** The plasmonic near-field significantly enhances these transitions, with optimal enhancement factors ($\eta$) observed at specific core radii ($\sim 80-100$ nm) that match the spatial scales of highly excited Rydberg states.

**Method.** The study employs theoretical modeling combining near-field electrodynamics (FDTD/analytical solutions) with quantum mechanical descriptions of excitons, utilizing averaging techniques for large excitons.

**Model / system.** The system involves core-shell nanostructures (e.g., Cu2O excitons) where the excitons interact with localized surface plasmons. The exciton states are modeled using modified Schrödinger-like equations leading to Whittaker equations.

**Key observables.** Local field gradient ($
abla\vec{E}$), transition rate ($\gamma$) for quadrupole transitions, and the plasmonic amplification factor ($\eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rydberg principal quantum number ($n$), core radius ($r$), and the matching condition between plasmon resonance and exciton resonance frequencies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the near-field approximation, the use of an averaging method for large excitons, and the assumption of zero confinement potential inside the nanostructure volume for simplified solutions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the calculated electric field intensity and the averaged local field amplification ($	ilde{\eta}$) as a function of the core radius, highlighting optimal enhancement regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical interaction (plasmon-exciton coupling) to deriving the necessary mathematical frameworks (Hamiltonians, differential equations like the Whittaker equation) and finally presenting numerical results showing the size-dependent enhancement of quadrupole transitions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interaction between Rydberg excitons in core-shell Cu$_2$O nanostructures and surface plasmons is investigated. An enhancement of a quadrupole transitions to the Rydberg exciton states in copper oxide plasmonic nanaostructures is examined. A possibility of a modification of plasmonic enhancment (i.e., an average amplification factor) of electric field distributions in the vicinity of nanostructures, which depends on its shape and geometrical size, is also discussed.

</details>

**Summary.** This work details the electrochemical synthesis of superconducting BKBO crystals, demonstrating that upgrading from simple two-electrode setups to a precise three-electrode potentiostatic system is crucial. This advanced control allows researchers to achieve highly homogeneous crystals exhibiting a full volume Meissner effect and a sharp superconducting transition. The combination of magnetic and electrochemical diagnostics provides a powerful pathway for tuning superconducting properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity, the rigorous application of electrochemical control (potentiostats, EIS) to tune material stoichiometry and crystal homogeneity mirrors techniques used in controlling interfaces in quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to synthesize high-quality, single-crystal superconducting $	ext{Ba}_{1-x}	ext{K}_x	ext{BiO}_3$ (BKBO) crystals exhibiting a full volume Meissner effect with a sharp superconducting transition.

**Main result.** Implementing a comprehensive three-electrode potentiostatic setup significantly improved crystal quality, leading to a markedly sharper superconducting transition and achieving a full Meissner response.

**Method.** The study employed electrochemical synthesis (anodic electrocrystallization) and characterized the results using magnetic susceptibility measurements and in-situ Electrochemical Impedance Spectroscopy (EIS).

**Model / system.** The physical system is the perovskite superconductor $	ext{Ba}_{1-x}	ext{K}_x	ext{BiO}_3$ (BKBO). The growth is controlled electrochemically, comparing basic two-electrode constant current methods against advanced three-electrode potentiostatic control.

**Key observables.** Magnetic susceptibility ($\chi$) across the superconducting transition, superconducting transition width, Meissner phase volume fraction, and EIS parameters ($R_{ct}$, $Q_{dl}$, CPE exponent).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Growth temperature ($225^\circ	ext{C}$), applied overpotential/voltage, and the control mechanism transitioning from two-electrode to three-electrode potentiostatic control.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The interpretation of $T_c$ alone is insufficient to uniquely determine potassium content, and the EIS analysis assumes interface changes are slow enough for local measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare magnetic susceptibility ($\chi$) for different growth methods, showing the progressive sharpening of the transition. Other figures detail the electrochemical setups and the Equivalent Circuit Model (ECM) used for EIS fitting.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares three electrochemical growth setups (Method 1, 2, and 3). It uses magnetic measurements to assess superconducting quality and employs in-situ EIS analysis to diagnose the underlying electrochemical processes governing nucleation and growth kinetics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work we show the results of electrochemical synthesis of BKBO crystals using three different experimental setup configurations. The setups differ between each other by varying level of control over physical variables and progressing from a two-electrode to a three-electrode configuration using highly oriented platinum counter electrode. By systematic analysis of the temperature dependence of the magnetic susceptibility at the superconducting transition of the collected crystals we observe that an order of magnitude sharper superconducting transition is achieved for the most comprehensive three-electrode setup. In addition, the level of chemical substitution can be controlled by the value of the overpotential versus reference electrode. We demonstrate that with this technique a full volume Meissner effect can be achieved with a sharp transition temperature for the superconducting crystal.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper models how influenza evolves by tracking antigenic drift within an eco-evolutionary SIR framework. It shows that the rate of evolution and the disease's prevalence are intrinsically linked, predicting that higher prevalence slows down antigenic change. The mathematical analysis uses advanced PDE techniques to derive these key ecological trade-offs.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the subject is epidemiology, the mathematical machinery—solving nonlocal PDEs for traveling wave solutions and deriving asymptotic scaling laws—shares techniques with non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and pattern formation in complex systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the joint determination of influenza A's prevalence and its rate of antigenic evolution, recognizing that these two ecological and evolutionary processes are coupled.

**Main result.** The speed of antigenic evolution decreases as prevalence rises, and the total number of infected hosts is maximized at an intermediate virulence level under certain conditions.

**Method.** The authors use asymptotic analysis to construct traveling pulse solutions to nonlocal partial differential equations describing the system's dynamics.

**Model / system.** The model is an eco-evolutionary SIR framework tracking infected and recovered hosts as distributions over a one-dimensional antigenic space, incorporating cross-immunity and potential host mortality.

**Key observables.** Speed of antigenic evolution, prevalence ($I_{asy}$), antigenic variance ($\sigma^2_{asy}$), and total number of infected hosts.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Virulence ($\omega$), host birth rate, immune evasion rate ($\epsilon$), and infection duration parameter ($m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption of a one-dimensional antigenic space and uses asymptotic approximations valid in specific parameter regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon an established SIR model, analyzes the endemic state as a traveling pulse, and derives asymptotic relationships between key epidemiological and evolutionary parameters by solving the underlying nonlocal PDEs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Influenza A persists by continually changing the antigens that its host population recognizes. How fast it evolves and how many hosts it infects are not independent, because the immunity that selects new variants is produced by the infections the pathogen has already caused. We analyze a model of Andreasen et.\ al.\ that captures this eco-evolutionary feedback, and we extend it to the case where infection may kill the host. In the model infected and recovered hosts are distributions over a one-dimensional antigenic space coupled by cross-immunity. The endemic state is a traveling pulse: the infected distribution keeps a fixed shape and advances through antigenic space at constant speed. In the limit of weak antigenic mutation we construct the pulse asymptotically and find that: (i) the speed of antigenic evolution decreases as prevalence rises, (ii) less standing antigenic variation accompanies either faster or slower antigenic evolution, depending on whether pathogens differ in immune evasion or host lifespan (iii) the total number of infected hosts is largest at an intermediate virulence when the host birth rate is sufficiently low.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work proposes using a dual-gated junction to electrically probe the hidden magnetic order in Anti-altermagnets (AAMs). By applying an electric field, the system's spin degeneracy is lifted, causing a measurable, asymmetric tunnel magnetoresistance (TMR). The findings establish a tunable spintronic framework for exploiting these novel magnetic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work connects magnetic order, symmetry breaking via electric fields, and transport signatures, which are core concepts in understanding emergent quantum phenomena in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop an all-electrical method to manipulate and probe the hidden magnetic order in Anti-altermagnets (AAMs), which possess a globally spin-degenerate band structure.

**Main result.** The study shows that applying an electric field breaks the spin degeneracy, leading to a strongly asymmetric tunnel magnetoresistance (TMR), while specific crystallographic orientations can restore symmetry due to momentum-space compensation.

**Method.** Quantum transport calculations using the Landauer formalism applied to a tight-binding Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system is an Anti-altermagnet (AAM) sandwiched between two ferromagnetic (FM) leads, configured in a dual-gated transport junction. The magnetic order is characterized by opposite spin-splitting in adjacent layers.

**Key observables.** Tunnel Magnetoresistance (TMR) as a function of the lead magnetization ($b$) and the applied gate-induced displacement field ($\Delta\mu$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Perpendicular displacement field ($\Delta\mu$), Rashba spin-orbit coupling ($\alpha$), and crystallographic orientation ($\phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the dual-gated FM-AAM-FM device architecture and the specific Hamiltonian terms governing the magnetic exchange and SOC.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic transport setup, TMR vs. Zeeman field showing asymmetry/symmetry dependence on angle, and momentum-space contours detailing the splitting and compensation effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework for probing AAMs via transport, demonstrating how gate-induced splitting causes TMR asymmetry, then analyzing the conditions (like specific angles or SOC strength) under which symmetry is maintained or enhanced.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Anti-altermagnets (AAMs) are a recently identified class of layered magnetic materials where opposite spin-splitting in adjacent layers creates a globally spin-degenerate band structure, rendering conventional spectroscopic detection highly difficult. In this paper, we theoretically demonstrate an all-electrical method to manipulate and probe this hidden magnetic order using a dual-gated transport junction. By applying a perpendicular displacement field, we break the spatial inversion symmetry of the lattice, explicitly lifting the global spin degeneracy. We attach ferromagnetic (FM) leads on either side of the AAM. Using quantum transport calculations, we show that this gate-induced spin-splitting manifests as a strongly asymmetric tunnel magnetoresistance (TMR) as a function of the lead magnetization. We identify specific crystallographic orientations where the TMR retains its symmetry despite the fully split bands, a direct consequence of exact momentum-space compensation. We further reveal that Rashba spin-orbit coupling guarantees robust, highly directional transport asymmetries even in the absence of an explicit chemical potential mismatch between the layers. Our findings establish a clear, electrically tunable framework for exploiting AAMs in next-generation spintronic architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the fundamental limits on the Chern number in lattice models by restricting the hopping interactions to a finite range. By analyzing the interplay between the number of Dirac points and the allowed mass sign patterns, the authors establish sharp upper bounds. The key finding is that the 13th-neighbor hopping can achieve a Chern number (51) that surpasses bounds set by shorter-range interactions, providing a design principle for high-Chern band engineering.

**Why it may be interesting.** The geometric viewpoint provided for constraining the Chern number via mass-sign patterns offers a general design principle applicable to moiré systems and other lattice models relevant to topological physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the maximum absolute Chern number achievable in a lattice model when the hopping amplitudes are restricted to a finite range.

**Main result.** The maximal Chern numbers found for 11th, 12th, and 13th-neighbor hopping are 36, 40, and 51, respectively, with the 13th-neighbor case exceeding previous theoretical bounds.

**Method.** The analysis uses algebraic zero counting and mass-weighted sum rules derived from the Hamiltonian structure to establish global bounds on the Chern number.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes a finite-range extension of the Qi--Wu--Zhang model on a square lattice, characterized by a Hamiltonian involving hybridization and mass terms dependent on hopping neighbors.

**Key observables.** Chern number ($|C|$), number of Dirac points ($N_0$), and the mass sign pattern at these points.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The hopping range, specified by the maximum neighbor shell considered (e.g., 11th, 12th, 13th neighbor).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The constraints are derived by considering how finite-range hopping limits the independent contributions of the mass terms to the total Chern number.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares calculated $|C|_{	ext{max}}$ values for different neighbor shells, while Figure 2 illustrates the zero-counting process for the fifth-neighbor case.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem, derives theoretical bounds using zero counting and mass constraints, calculates the maximum Chern numbers for increasing hopping ranges, and discusses the implications of these bounds being broken by larger hopping ranges.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Finite-range hopping constrains both the number of Dirac points and the masses that gap them. We determine the maximal absolute Chern numbers of an isotropic finite-range extension of the Qi--Wu--Zhang model on a square lattice through thirteenth-neighbor hopping. Algebraic zero counting and mass-weighted sum rules yield global bounds, and we construct explicit Hamiltonians that saturate them. We find $|C|_{\max}=36,40,51$ for eleventh-, twelfth-, and thirteenth-neighbor hopping. Equal numbers of Dirac points can produce different maxima because finite-range mass harmonics distinguish opposite vortex charges differently. Twice the squared hopping radius bounds every maximum through twelfth-neighbor hopping and is saturated at the eleventh and twelfth, but the thirteenth-neighbor maximum exceeds it. Thus finite-range hopping constrains the maximal Chern number in two complementary ways: through the number of Dirac vortices it can generate and through the mass-sign patterns it can assign to them. This geometric viewpoint provides a design principle for high-Chern bands and a route to analogous bounds in moiré and other lattice systems.

</details>

<sub>[↑ back to top](#top)</sub>

</details>
